This reference synthesizes monitoring recommendations from authoritative associations for the CMS CCW 30 chronic conditions. It is a practice planning and education resource for administrators, physicians, and providers. Every recommendation is footnoted to its source so it can be verified and updated as guidelines evolve. Clinical decisions for individual patients remain with the treating clinician.

CMID — Clinical Monitoring Intelligence Database

A reference work of monitoring recommendations for the 30 chronic conditions tracked by the CMS Chronic Conditions Data Warehouse (CCW). One monograph per condition. Every recommendation is footnoted back to the originating association and source document so the content can be updated as guidelines evolve.

This is a practice planning and education resource for administrators, physicians, and providers. It does not replace clinical judgment, define the totality of service for any patient, or generate patient-specific recommendations.

What's here

cmid/ ├── README.md this file ├── index.md table of contents — conditions by prevalence and by category ├── bibliography.md master list of cited associations + source documents with URLs ├── conditions/ │ ├── _template.md the template every condition file follows │ ├── 01-hypertension.md │ ├── 02-hyperlipidemia.md │ └── ... (30 files, numbered by 2022 CCW prevalence rank) ├── render.py build script — produces out/cmid.html └── out/ └── cmid.html rendered single-file reference; the artifact for sharing

November 2026 update

All 30 monographs were re-checked on 2026-11-23 against association guidelines, USPSTF statements, CMS coverage rules, CDC vaccine guidance and FDA labels published since the 2026-05-11 build. Superseded sources were replaced and footnoted; conflicts are flagged in each Notes section. Items that could only be confirmed from secondary coverage were left unchanged. Major source changes:

  • Cardiovascular / metabolic — 2025 AHA/ACC High Blood Pressure guideline (PREVENT risk, UACR for all hypertension, aldosterone:renin in resistant hypertension); 2026 ACC/AHA Dyslipidemia guideline applied across lipid, IHD, AMI and stroke; ADA Standards of Care 2026; AACE 2026 diabetes algorithm; 2026 ACC HFpEF pathway; cardiac rehabilitation now under 42 CFR §410.49 (NCD 20.10 retired).
  • Kidney / blood — KDIGO 2026 Anemia in CKD (final) replaces KDIGO 2012; ASH 2026 iron-deficiency guideline.
  • Respiratory — GOLD 2027 report; GINA 2026; ATS 2025 CAP partial update.
  • Oncology — ASCO 2026 breast cancer surveillance tiers; AUA/ASTRO/SUO 2026 prostate amendments; AUA/SUO 2026 NMIBC amendment; current NCCN versions for lung, uterine, kidney.
  • Other — AUA 2026 BPH guideline; AAO 2025 hydroxychloroquine screening and 2026 eye-care PPPs; ATA 2026 thyroid-in-pregnancy; 2026 AHA/ASA stroke rehabilitation; lecanemab (2026) and donanemab label changes; IGSLi/ISBD 2026 lithium-kidney guidance.
  • Vaccines — CDC pneumococcal (all adults ≥50), RSV (≥75, or 50–74 at increased risk) and 2026–27 COVID-19 guidance. Federal vaccine recommendations remain subject to litigation (AAP v. Kennedy stay, March 2026); re-verify at each refresh.

Note: this update was made directly in the rendered HTML; the markdown source files were not available and have not been updated.

Synthesis rule

When multiple associations issue overlapping recommendations, the synthesized recommendation follows this authority order:

  1. CMS / federal agency (CMS, USPSTF, CDC, NIH/NHLBI/NIDDK/NCI)
  2. USPSTF specifically for screening intervals
  3. Specialty professional society (AHA/ACC, ADA, ASN, etc.)
  4. Disease foundation (NKF, ALA, Arthritis Foundation, etc.)

Conflicts are flagged, never silently resolved — the disagreement itself is often the signal a clinician needs. When sources agree on an item but disagree on interval, the more conservative (more frequent) interval wins.

Updating

Recommendations change. To update:

  1. Edit the relevant conditions/NN-name.md file.
  2. Update or add the citation in bibliography.md.
  3. Re-run python render.py to regenerate out/cmid.html.

Markdown is the source of truth. HTML is the artifact.

Rendering

pip install markdown python render.py

Open out/cmid.html in any browser.

Index

CMS CCW 30 chronic conditions, 2022 Medicare FFS prevalence (n ≈ 28.6M beneficiaries). Source: CCW Table B.2.a, April 2024.

Click any condition to open its full monograph (prose, footnoted recommendations, source citations).

Hypertension

ICD-10: I10 (essential), I11–I15 (secondary/complicated forms) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 66.8% — rank 1 of 30 Category: Metabolic / Cardiovascular Primary associations consulted: ACC/AHA, USPSTF, NHLBI, CMS, CDC

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — — BMP, K+, eGFR* Lipid panel, UACR, fasting glucose / HbA1c — TSH; aldosterone:renin ratio (resistant HTN; consider stage 2 / OSA)
Imaging — — — — — ECG (baseline), Echo (Stage 2 or LVH), Renal US (resistant HTN)
Physical exam BP, weight / BMI, cardiac auscultation — — Fundoscopic exam ABI / peripheral pulse (if ≥65 or smoker) —
Functional / surveys — — — PHQ-9, 10-yr CVD risk (PREVENT) — —

* every 6 months if on ACE/ARB, diuretic, or MRA; otherwise annually.

Summary recommendation

Adults with diagnosed hypertension should have blood pressure measured at every clinical encounter, supplemented by out-of-office measurement (home BP monitoring or 24-hour ambulatory BP monitoring) at diagnosis and when treatment changes.12 The 2025 AHA/ACC target is <130/80 mmHg for all adults with hypertension (additional considerations for institutional care, limited life expectancy, and pregnancy).11 Baseline laboratory evaluation screens for secondary causes and end-organ damage; routine surveillance focuses on renal function and electrolytes (especially in patients on RAAS-acting agents or diuretics), lipid status, glycemic status, and urinary albumin. 10-year total CVD risk is estimated with the AHA PREVENT equations (ages 30–79; replaces Pooled Cohort Equations); in stage 1 hypertension, drug therapy starts at PREVENT ≥7.5% or with clinical CVD, diabetes, or CKD, otherwise after 3–6 months of lifestyle therapy fails to reach <130/80.11 Echocardiography is reserved for new Stage 2 hypertension, suspected left ventricular hypertrophy, or clinical change. The most conservative interval among overlapping recommendations wins when sources disagree on cadence.

Laboratory

  • Basic Metabolic Panel (BMP) — annually if stable; every 6 months if on ACE inhibitor, ARB, or diuretic. Monitors potassium, sodium, creatinine, and bicarbonate.1
  • eGFR / serum creatinine — every 6 months on RAAS therapy or in patients with CKD; otherwise annually. Detects nephropathy and guides medication dosing.1
  • Serum potassium — every 6 months on potassium-affecting therapy (ACE/ARB, diuretic, MRA). Target 3.5–5.0 mmol/L.1
  • Fasting lipid panel — annually; informs concurrent ASCVD risk-driven statin decisions.1
  • Urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio (UACR) — at diagnosis in every adult with hypertension (Class 1 in 2025; optional in 2017); repeat annually. ACR ≥30 mg/g indicates end-organ involvement.1
  • Fasting glucose or HbA1c — annually. Hypertension and dysglycemia frequently co-occur and concurrent diabetes lowers the BP target and intensifies monitoring.1
  • TSH — at diagnosis and as clinically indicated; consider in resistant hypertension to rule out thyroid contribution.1
  • Plasma aldosterone-to-renin ratio (primary aldosteronism screen) — all adults with resistant hypertension, regardless of serum K+ (Class 1); consider in stage 2 hypertension and obstructive sleep apnea. Continue most antihypertensives during testing; hold MRAs.1

Imaging

  • 12-lead ECG — at diagnosis (baseline LVH and rhythm assessment); repeat as clinically indicated.1
  • Echocardiogram — at diagnosis if Stage 2 hypertension or suspected LVH; otherwise as indicated by clinical change.1
  • Renal ultrasound or renal artery imaging — only when resistant hypertension or secondary cause suspected.1

Physical examination

  • Blood pressure — every clinical encounter, using validated technique (seated, after 5 min rest, appropriate cuff size, average of two readings).12
  • Out-of-office BP (home BP monitoring or ABPM) — recommended to confirm the office diagnosis and to monitor and titrate therapy as part of an integrated care plan; USPSTF also requires out-of-office confirmation before diagnosis.12
  • Postpartum history of pregnancy-associated hypertension — BP at least annually.1
  • Weight and BMI — every visit; a 5–10 lb weight reduction lowers SBP ~4–5 mmHg.1
  • Cardiac auscultation — every visit; assess for S3, S4, murmurs.1
  • Peripheral pulse and ABI screening — every 2–3 years in patients ≥65 or with smoking history; screens for PAD.1
  • Fundoscopic exam — annually in chronic hypertension; assesses hypertensive retinopathy.1

Functional / surveys

  • 10-year total CVD risk (AHA PREVENT, ages 30–79; replaces Pooled Cohort Equations) — at diagnosis and periodically. PREVENT ≥7.5% triggers drug therapy in stage 1 HTN; also informs statin decisions (see Hyperlipidemia).1
  • PHQ-9 depression screen — annually; hypertension and depression are bidirectionally associated.1

Notes

  • Screening vs. surveillance. USPSTF (2021) addresses screening in asymptomatic adults ≥18 — annual screening if ≥40 or at increased risk, every 3–5 years for normotensive 18–39 year-olds at average risk — and requires out-of-office confirmation before diagnosis. AHA/ACC (2025; replaced 2017) covers post-diagnosis management and surveillance.121
  • BP target disagreement. AHA/ACC (2025) sets <130/80 for all adults, with additional considerations for institutional care, limited life expectancy, and pregnancy. ADA 2026 encourages SBP <120 in high CV/kidney risk and allows <140/90 in older adults in poor health. Per the synthesis rule, the lower target is reflected here; individualize in frail older adults.13
  • Guideline currency. The 2025 AHA/ACC guideline (Aug 2025) retired the 2017 guideline and is a living document (errata through Jun 2026); verify focused updates at refresh.1

Footnotes

Hypertension. 2025;82(10):e212-e316. doi:10.1161/HYP.0000000000000249.   3. American Diabetes Association Professional Practice Committee for Diabetes. Standards of Care in Diabetes—2026. Diabetes Care. 2026;49(Suppl 1). Summary of Revisions: S6-S12. doi:10.2337/dc26-SREV. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12690167/ See bibliography.md → ada-soc-2026.

Hyperlipidemia

ICD-10: E78.5 (unspecified), E78.0 (pure hypercholesterolemia), E78.1 (hypertriglyceridemia), E78.2 (mixed) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 65.5% — rank 2 of 30 Category: Metabolic Primary associations consulted: ACC/AHA, USPSTF, NLA, AACE, NHLBI

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — Lipid panel during titration (4–12 wk after start or change) — Lipid panel (stable patient), HbA1c (statin-associated DM surveillance) — Lp(a) once in adulthood, apoB (residual-risk question), ALT baseline (pre-statin), CK baseline (high SAMS risk only)
Imaging — — — — — CAC score — men ≥40 / women ≥45, PREVENT 3–<10%, when statin decision uncertain (Class 1)
Physical exam BP, weight / BMI, smoking status — — — — —
Functional / surveys Adherence review, diet / lifestyle counseling — — — — PREVENT-ASCVD 10/30-yr risk, ages 30–79, every 4–6 yr

Summary recommendation

Adults with diagnosed hyperlipidemia should have a follow-up lipid panel 4–12 weeks after starting or changing lipid-lowering therapy to confirm adherence and percent LDL-C reduction (≥50% on high-intensity statin, ≥30% on moderate-intensity), then every 3–12 months until stable, transitioning to annual surveillance once at goal on unchanged therapy.14 The 2026 ACC/AHA Dyslipidemia Guideline reintroduced explicit numeric LDL-C targets — <55 mg/dL for very-high-risk ASCVD, <70 mg/dL for high risk, <100 mg/dL for borderline/intermediate — and added one-time universal Lp(a) measurement as a Class I recommendation.2 Intensification (ezetimibe, then PCSK9 inhibitor, bempedoic acid, or inclisiran) is triggered when LDL-C remains above the risk-stratified threshold despite maximally tolerated statin. Routine ALT and CK surveillance is not recommended; check ALT once before statin initiation and CK only at baseline in patients at increased SAMS risk, then symptom-driven thereafter.1 Each visit should include adherence review, lifestyle counseling, BP/BMI/smoking assessment, and periodic recalculation of 10-year ASCVD risk using the AHA PREVENT equations.

Laboratory

  • Lipid panel (fasting or non-fasting) — 4–12 weeks after statin initiation or dose change to assess adherence and percent LDL-C reduction; every 3–12 months until stable; then annually.14
  • LDL-C against target — at each lipid panel. 2026 numeric thresholds: <55 mg/dL very-high-risk ASCVD, <70 mg/dL high risk, <100 mg/dL borderline/intermediate. 2018 ACC/AHA used percent-reduction goals plus threshold triggers (≥70 mg/dL, ≥100 mg/dL).21
  • Non-HDL cholesterol (calculated) — at each lipid panel. 2026 goals: <130 mg/dL borderline/intermediate risk; <100 mg/dL high risk (≈30 mg/dL above the LDL-C goal).2
  • Apolipoprotein B (apoB) — when LDL-C and non-HDL-C goals are met, to assess residual risk in CKM syndrome, type 2 diabetes, hypertriglyceridemia, or known ASCVD; elevated apoB may justify LLT intensification even at LDL-C / non-HDL-C goal.2
  • Lipoprotein(a) [Lp(a)] — at least once in adulthood (Class I in 2026); repeat generally not needed. ≥125 nmol/L (≥50 mg/dL) ≈1.4-fold and ≥250 nmol/L ≥2-fold long-term MI/stroke risk; elevated Lp(a) → intensify LDL-C lowering and risk-factor control.2
  • ALT (hepatic transaminases) — baseline once before statin initiation; routine surveillance not recommended without symptoms.1
  • CK (creatine kinase) — baseline only in patients at elevated SAMS risk; otherwise symptom-driven.1
  • HbA1c or fasting glucose — periodically per diabetes-screening guidance; statin therapy is continued despite incident DM, but the new diagnosis warrants management.13

Imaging

  • Coronary artery calcium (CAC) score (noncontrast) — Class 1 (upgraded from 2a) for men ≥40 and women ≥45 with borderline or intermediate PREVENT risk (3–<10%) when the result would change the statin decision. CAC = 0 may support deferral in low/intermediate risk without other comorbidity; any CAC >0 → LDL-C goal <100 mg/dL, lower goals at higher scores.2

Physical examination

  • Blood pressure, weight / BMI, waist circumference, smoking status — at each clinical encounter as part of cardiovascular risk reassessment.1

Functional / surveys

  • Adherence and lifestyle assessment — at each follow-up visit. Heart-healthy diet, ≥150 minutes/week moderate-intensity activity, tobacco cessation; shared decision-making about therapy is Class I.1
  • 10-year (and 30-year) ASCVD risk (AHA PREVENT-ASCVD) — adults 30–79 without known ASCVD or subclinical atherosclerosis and LDL-C 70–189 mg/dL; recalculate every 4–6 years. PREVENT replaces Pooled Cohort Equations in the 2026 guideline. Risk strata: <3% low, 3–<5% borderline, 5–<10% intermediate, ≥10% high.2

Notes

  • 2026 ACC/AHA Dyslipidemia Guideline (published Mar 13, 2026; Circulation/JACC) replaces the 2018 Cholesterol Guideline. Key changes: numeric LDL-C goals (<55 very-high-risk ASCVD / <70 high risk / <100 borderline-intermediate) with paired non-HDL-C goals (<100 / <130); one-time universal Lp(a); selective apoB; PREVENT-ASCVD (ages 30–79) replaces Pooled Cohort Equations with new strata (<3 / 3–<5 / 5–<10 / ≥10%); CAC upgraded to Class 1.2
  • USPSTF vs. ACC/AHA scope mismatch. USPSTF 2022 addresses statin initiation for primary prevention only and is silent on monitoring cadence. Clinicians cannot derive surveillance intervals from USPSTF — ACC/AHA, NLA, and AACE fill that gap.31
  • Risk-tool conflict (federal vs specialty). USPSTF 2022 statin grades use Pooled Cohort Equations (Grade B ≥10%, Grade C 7.5–<10%, ages 40–75 with ≥1 risk factor); ACC/AHA 2026 uses PREVENT with intermediate risk 5–<10%. Thresholds are not interchangeable; federal precedence applies to coverage-linked preventive statin decisions — document which tool was used.32
  • Numeric targets vs. percent reduction. 2018 ACC/AHA emphasized percent LDL-C reduction; AACE 2017 and 2025 retained numeric goals; 2026 ACC/AHA returned to explicit numeric goals — US guidance now aligns more closely with international targets.562
  • Chronic-monitoring interval disagreement. ACC/AHA: "every 3–12 months as needed." NLA: "every 12 months" for stable patients. Practical synthesis (conservative wins): annual lipid panel for stable patients; quarterly during titration.14

Footnotes

  1. Grundy SM et al. 2018 AHA/ACC Guideline on the Management of Blood Cholesterol. Circulation. 2019;139(25):e1082-e1143. See bibliography.md → accaha-chol-2018. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

  2. Blumenthal RS, Morris PB, Gaudino M, et al. 2026 ACC/AHA/AACVPR/ABC/ACPM/ADA/AGS/APhA/ASPC/NLA/PCNA Guideline on the Management of Dyslipidemia. Circulation. 2026;153(17):e1154-e1276. doi:10.1161/CIR.0000000000001423. Also J Am Coll Cardiol. 2026;87(19):2624-2757. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41824552/ See bibliography.md → accaha-dyslip-2026. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

  3. USPSTF. Statin Use for the Primary Prevention of Cardiovascular Disease in Adults: Recommendation Statement. JAMA. 2022;328(8):746-753. See bibliography.md → uspstf-statin-2022. ↩↩

Rheumatoid Arthritis / Osteoarthritis

ICD-10: RA — M05.x, M06.x. OA — M15–M19. CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 36.2% — rank 3 of 30 (combined; CCW does not separate) Category: Musculoskeletal Primary associations consulted: ACR, EULAR, USPSTF (limited), NIAMS, Arthritis Foundation, AAO (HCQ retinopathy)

RA and OA are distinct diseases combined into a single CCW category. They share little in monitoring approach and are addressed separately below.

Rheumatoid Arthritis

At-a-glance schedule (RA)

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — CBC, ALT/AST, Cr (DMARD safety after stable); lipid panel + CBC at 4–8 wk then q3–6 mo on tocilizumab / sarilumab / JAK — TB screening (if ongoing exposure risk); vaccination status review (influenza, pneumococcal, RZV, RSV, COVID-19 [shared decision]) — TB / HBV / HCV pre-biologic; HBV DNA q1–3 mo if HBcAb+ on biologic without prophylaxis; baseline labs pre-DMARD initiation
Imaging — — — Hand / wrist / foot radiographs (every 1–2 years to track erosive progression) — —
Physical exam 28-joint tender / swollen joint count — — — — —
Functional / surveys Disease activity (DAS28-CRP, CDAI, SDAI, RAPID3, or PAS-II); HAQ-DI or MDHAQ Disease activity every 3 mo until target reached Disease activity every 6 mo in sustained remission HCQ: SD-OCT + FAF (baseline; annually — may defer yrs 1–5 if no risk factors) — —

Summary recommendation (RA)

Primary-care monitoring of RA follows a treat-to-target framework: at every visit (minimum every 3 months until low disease activity or remission, every 6 months thereafter), document a validated composite disease-activity score (DAS28-CRP, CDAI, SDAI, RAPID3, or PAS-II), a 28-joint count, and a functional assessment (HAQ-DI or MDHAQ).12 DMARD-safety labs (CBC, ALT/AST, creatinine) are obtained at baseline and every 2–4 weeks during the first 3 months of methotrexate, leflunomide, or sulfasalazine, every 8–12 weeks during months 3–6, then every 12 weeks; hydroxychloroquine requires only baseline labs; retinal screening (AAO 2025) is a baseline fundus + SD-OCT + wide-field fundus autofluorescence (FAF) soon after starting, then annual OCT + FAF while on HCQ (may be deferred during the first 5 years only if no significant risk factors), with dose ≤5.0 mg/kg real body weight/day (<400 mg/day in severe obesity)6.26 Biologics and JAK inhibitors require pre-treatment TB, HBV, and HCV screening; tocilizumab, sarilumab, and JAK inhibitors add lipid surveillance at 4–8 weeks and then every 3–6 months.2 Hand and foot radiographs at baseline and periodically (every 1–2 years) track erosive progression; vaccinations are reviewed annually.4

Laboratory (RA)

  • CBC, ALT/AST, serum creatinine — baseline; every 2–4 weeks during first 3 months of methotrexate, leflunomide, or sulfasalazine; every 8–12 weeks months 3–6; every 12 weeks thereafter.2
  • CBC, ALT/AST, Cr (intensified) — every 4–8 weeks during first 6 months, then every 3 months for patients with NAFLD or hepatic risk on methotrexate.1
  • Hydroxychloroquine monotherapy — baseline labs only; periodic only if clinically indicated.2
  • Lipid panel, ALT/AST, neutrophil count — 4–8 weeks after starting tocilizumab or sarilumab, then every 3–6 months (lipids every 24 weeks).2
  • Lipid panel, ALT/AST, CBC with neutrophils — 4–8 weeks after JAK inhibitor (tofacitinib, baricitinib, upadacitinib) initiation, then every 3 months.2
  • CBC, platelets — every 2–4 months on rituximab.2
  • TB (IGRA or TST), HBV (HBsAg, anti-HBc, anti-HBs), HCV antibody — baseline before any biologic or tsDMARD; annual TB screening if ongoing exposure risk.21
  • HBV DNA — every 1–3 months during and 6–12 months after biologic / JAK in HBcAb-positive patients without antiviral prophylaxis.1

Imaging (RA)

  • Plain radiographs of hands, wrists, feet — baseline; periodically (every 1–2 years) to assess erosive progression, particularly seropositive disease.7

Physical examination (RA)

  • 28-joint tender / swollen joint count — at every visit; minimum every 3 months until target; every 6 months in remission.12

Functional / surveys (RA)

  • Disease-activity measure (DAS28-CRP, CDAI, SDAI, RAPID3, or PAS-II) — every visit. Targets: ACR/EULAR Boolean 2.0 remission (TJC ≤1, SJC ≤1, CRP ≤1 mg/dL, PtGA ≤2 on 0–10 scale)8, or SDAI ≤3.3 / CDAI ≤2.8.1
  • Functional status (HAQ-DI or MDHAQ) — at each visit or at least annually.3
  • Vaccination review — annually (ACR 2022 + current CDC schedule): influenza annually; pneumococcal conjugate (PCV15/20/21) for all adults ≥50 and younger adults with risk conditions incl. immunosuppression; recombinant zoster; RSV (single dose ≥75; 50–74 if at increased risk); COVID-19 by individual-based (shared clinical) decision-making; Tdap/Td.410
  • HCQ retinal screening — baseline fundus + SD-OCT + wide-field FAF soon after initiation; annual SD-OCT + FAF while on HCQ (may defer during years 1–5 if no risk factors: dose >5 mg/kg real weight, renal disease, tamoxifen, older age at initiation). Visual fields (e.g., 24-2C) and mfERG are secondary confirmatory tests; screen for both parafoveal and pericentral patterns.6

Osteoarthritis

At-a-glance schedule (OA)

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — — — — — ALT (chronic acetaminophen); CBC / Cr / BP (chronic NSAID periodic surveillance)
Imaging — — — — — Plain radiographs — diagnostic uncertainty or surgical planning; not recommended for routine surveillance
Physical exam Joint exam (ROM, effusion, alignment, crepitus); BMI / weight in overweight knee or hip OA — — — — —
Functional / surveys Pain (NRS / VAS); function (WOMAC or KOOS knee; HOOS hip; AUSCAN / FIHOA hand); exercise / self-management adherence — — — — Depression / anxiety / sleep / coping screen

Summary recommendation (OA)

OA surveillance is symptom- and function-driven, not test-driven. At each visit reassess pain (NRS or VAS) and function using a validated instrument (WOMAC or KOOS for knee, HOOS for hip, AUSCAN or FIHOA for hand), confirm engagement in the strongly recommended exercise and self-management program, and (in knee or hip OA) measure BMI to track the clinically meaningful 5% weight-loss target.5 Hepatotoxicity surveillance with ALT is advised for chronic acetaminophen users, particularly at doses approaching 3 g/day; CBC, creatinine, and blood pressure should be checked periodically in chronic oral NSAID users.5 The 2019 ACR/AF guideline does not recommend routine surveillance imaging (not reversed by the 2026 ACR update, which strongly recommends ultrasound guidance for hip intra-articular glucocorticoid injection)11 — radiographs are reserved for diagnostic uncertainty or to evaluate worsening symptoms and surgical candidacy. Mental health, sleep, and coping should be screened given their high comorbid burden and direct effect on pain perception.5

Laboratory (OA)

  • ALT — periodically in chronic acetaminophen users, particularly at doses approaching 3 g/day.5
  • CBC, creatinine / eGFR, blood pressure — periodically (commonly every 6–12 months) in chronic oral NSAID users.5
  • Methotrexate for erosive hand OA (conditional; direction changed in 2026) — apply RA methotrexate safety labs: CBC, ALT/AST, creatinine at baseline; every 2–4 weeks first 3 months; every 8–12 weeks months 3–6; every 12 weeks thereafter. Methotrexate is conditionally recommended against for knee/hip OA.112

Imaging (OA)

  • Plain radiograph (weight-bearing knee, AP pelvis for hip, PA hand) — at diagnosis if uncertainty; not recommended for routine follow-up. Repeat only with clinical change or to evaluate surgical candidacy.5

Physical examination (OA)

  • Joint exam (range of motion, effusion, alignment, crepitus) — every visit.5
  • BMI and weight — each visit for overweight / obese hip and knee OA (weight loss strongly recommended); target ≥5% body-weight loss. GLP-1 RA conditionally recommended (new 2026) with diet and exercise in knee OA with obesity — monitor weight trajectory and GI tolerance.511

Functional / surveys (OA)

  • Pain (NRS or VAS) — each clinical encounter; reassess after each therapeutic trial.5
  • Function (WOMAC or KOOS for knee; HOOS for hip; AUSCAN or FIHOA for hand) — each clinical encounter.5
  • Self-management / exercise adherence; gait, balance, ADL screening — ongoing.5
  • Mental-health screening (depression, anxiety, sleep, coping) — periodically. CBT conditionally recommended for knee, hip, and hand OA (ACR 2026).511

Notes

  • Methotrexate lab cadence disagreement. EULAR (2019; RA management recommendations updated 2025) and some real-world data9 support every-3-month monitoring after stability; ACR / FDA labeling specifies every 8–12 weeks. Emerging evidence suggests 6-month intervals may be safe for stable patients but is not yet in ACR guidance — conservative cadence (every 8–12 weeks) wins.2
  • Disease-activity instrument choice. ACR endorses any of five measures without preference; EULAR prefers DAS28; primary-care literature favors RAPID3 for feasibility.1
  • HCQ retinopathy screening. AAO 2025 revision: OCT + FAF are primary tools (VF/mfERG confirmatory); annual screening is the default and may be deferred during years 1–5 only without risk factors. Conservative synthesis: baseline + annual from start when any risk factor is present.6
  • OA imaging. ACR/AF 2019 does not recommend routine follow-up radiographs; OARSI 2019 similarly de-emphasizes surveillance imaging. There is no major US guideline endorsing scheduled OA imaging surveillance, in contrast with common clinical practice; the ACR 2026 update (summary posted Sep 2026; full manuscript pending) does not address surveillance imaging.115
  • Acetaminophen for OA disagreement. ACR 2026 update retains a conditional recommendation for acetaminophen (knee, hip, hand), with hepatotoxicity monitoring as before; NICE 2022 (UK) recommends against routine acetaminophen — an internationally relevant divergence.11

Benign Prostatic Hyperplasia

ICD-10: N40.0, N40.1, N40.2, N40.3 CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 29.1% (males only) — rank 4 of 30 Category: Other (urologic; males only) Primary associations consulted: AUA, USPSTF (PSA screening only), NIDDK, AAFP, Urology Care Foundation

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — — — — — Urinalysis (baseline; repeat with new hematuria / UTI / worsening symptoms); PSA (baseline if would change management, pre–5-ARI, per shared decision for PCa screening — USPSTF every 2–4 yr if 55–69); PSA reassessment 6 mo after starting 5-ARI; serum creatinine (red-flag features only)
Imaging — — — — — Prostate volume / shape imaging before procedural intervention
Physical exam — — — DRE (NIDDK: annually or more often; AUA: baseline + clinical-judgment thereafter) — —
Functional / surveys IPSS (AUA-SI) at each follow-up visit — — IPSS annual reassessment (watchful waiting) — Post-void residual (PVR — retention; required pre-procedure; may be offered at follow-up); uroflowmetry (retention / exclude other disorders); response visit within 6 mo after initiating treatment; post-procedural follow-up for ≥1 yr

Summary recommendation

For a male with diagnosed BPH/LUTS, surveillance is built around the IPSS administered at baseline and at every follow-up; mild or non-bothersome symptoms (IPSS ≤7) are managed by watchful waiting with annual reassessment including symptom score, focused history, urinalysis as indicated, and DRE.134 After initiating treatment, patients should be re-evaluated within 6 months with standardized symptom scores and adverse-event review; without improvement, pursue an alternative diagnosis and/or treatment pathway. On a 5-ARI,1 with a new PSA baseline established at ~6 months and measured PSA doubled thereafter for cancer-risk interpretation.1 Worsening IPSS, elevated or rising PVR, recurrent UTI, hematuria, retention, renal impairment, or stones trigger urology referral. PSA in the BPH-monitored patient serves two distinct purposes that must not be conflated: a BPH-management role (estimates prostate volume; prostate volume >30 cc and/or PSA >1.5 ng/mL defines 5-ARI candidacy)1 and a prostate-cancer screening role (USPSTF restricts to shared decision-making in men 55–69 every 2–4 years; recommends against in men ≥70).12

Laboratory

  • Urinalysis — baseline; repeat as indicated (new hematuria, dysuria, suspected UTI, worsening symptoms).1
  • PSA — may be obtained at the initial evaluation after shared decision-making (Expert Opinion). 5-ARI candidacy: prostate volume >30 cc and/or PSA >1.5 ng/mL; counsel on adverse effects and the impact on prostate-cancer diagnosis. Repeat per shared-decision PCa screening cadence (USPSTF: every 2–4 years for ages 55–69).12
  • PSA reassessment on 5-ARI — baseline before starting; repeat at ~6 months to establish new nadir, then periodically. Expect ~50% reduction by 6 months; any rise above nadir warrants urologic evaluation; double measured PSA for cancer-risk interpretation.1
  • Serum creatinine — as indicated (not routine). Obtain with elevated PVR, hydronephrosis, recurrent UTI, hematuria, or suspected obstructive uropathy.1

Imaging

No routine surveillance imaging. Before a procedural intervention, use imaging to assess the volume and shape of the prostate (Clinical Principle). Cystoscopy may be performed before a procedure (Clinical Principle), and may be offered with pressure-flow testing / UDS if LUTS worsen during follow-up (Expert Opinion).1

Physical examination

  • Digital rectal examination (DRE) — baseline. NIDDK patient guidance states "annually or more often" during ongoing BPH care; AUA treats DRE as a baseline assessment with repetition driven by clinical change (reliably detects glands >50 mL).134

Functional / surveys

  • IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score / AUA-SI) — baseline; every follow-up visit; annual minimum in watchful waiting. Bands: mild 0–7, moderate 8–19, severe ≥20. A ≥3–4-point change is clinically meaningful and triggers treatment reassessment.1
  • Post-void residual (PVR) — may be obtained to evaluate for retention or exclude other disorders (Clinical Principle); may be offered during routine follow-up (Expert Opinion); required before a procedural intervention (Clinical Principle).1
  • Uroflowmetry — optional at baseline; may be repeated at follow-up (4–12 weeks post-treatment) or before surgical decision. Qmax <10 mL/s suggests bladder outlet obstruction.1
  • Treatment-response reassessment — within 6 months after initiating treatment: standardized symptom score (IPSS) + adverse events. Without improvement → pursue alternative diagnosis and/or treatment pathway.1
  • Watchful-waiting reassessment — annually for men with mild symptoms (IPSS ≤7) or non-bothersome moderate symptoms.14
  • Post-procedural follow-up (TURP, PVP, HoLEP / ThuLEP, WVTT / Rezum, PUL / UroLift, PAE, TIPD) — evaluate for at least 1 year to assess response (standardized symptom score + adverse events; PVR and uroflowmetry may be assessed).1

Notes

  • PSA frequency in the BPH patient — conflicting frames. AUA endorses offering PSA at BPH workup when it would change management (including before 5-ARI) and supports periodic monitoring during 5-ARI therapy. USPSTF frames PSA strictly as asymptomatic screening every 2–4 years for ages 55–69, against in men ≥70 — USPSTF does not address symptomatic BPH patients. The gap is closed via shared decision-making. The USPSTF prostate-cancer screening topic is being updated (final research plan Dec 2023); the 2018 statement remains in force.212
  • DRE cadence disagreement. NIDDK patient material says "annually or more often." AUA treats DRE as baseline with clinical-judgment repetition. AAFP notes limited routine utility (reliable only for glands >50 mL).134
  • PVR and uroflowmetry as routine surveillance. AUA 2026: may be obtained to evaluate for retention or exclude other disorders and may be offered during routine follow-up (Expert Opinion); PVR is required before a procedural intervention. AAFP 2023 notes PVR is not useful for routine monitoring. Conservative synthesis: not routine annually; obtain with worsening symptoms and pre-procedure.14

Footnotes

  1. Goueli R, Badlani GH, Welliver C, Anderson PD, et al. Management of Lower Urinary Tract Symptoms Attributed to Benign Prostatic Hyperplasia: AUA Guideline (2026). Part I: Presentation and Evaluation. Diabetes Mellitus (Type 2)

ICD-10: E11.x (Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus, with or without complications) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 26.4% — rank 5 of 30 Category: Metabolic Primary associations consulted: ADA, AACE, USPSTF, KDIGO, AGS, CMS, NIDDK

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs CGM review (if on CGM; CGM recommended if on insulin / hypoglycemia-causing agents) HbA1c (if not at goal or after therapy change) HbA1c (stable, at goal); Cr / eGFR (CKD G3); 4–12 wk lipid panel post statin start / change UACR, Cr / eGFR (no CKD), lipid panel (all; on or off statin), ALT / AST + FIB-4 (MASLD screen), TSH (periodic) — B12 (metformin >5 yr); K+ (ACEi/ARB/MRA at start + periodically; finerenone at 1 mo; diuretic 7–14 d after start/dose change); risk-tier CKD labs (G3 q6–12 mo, G4 q3–5 mo, G5 q1–3 mo)
Imaging — — — — — DXA q2–3 yr (≥65, or younger with multiple fracture risk factors)
Physical exam BP, weight / BMI / waist, hypoglycemia review — — Comprehensive foot exam, dilated eye exam (q1–2 yr if no retinopathy and at glycemic goal; annual if retinopathy), distal polyneuropathy assessment (10-g monofilament + pinprick / temperature / vibration), dental exam — Foot exam every visit if prior ulcer / sensory loss
Functional / surveys Smoking / tobacco / e-cigarette + cessation counseling — — ASCVD 10-yr risk (PREVENT), PHQ-2 / PHQ-9, GAD-7, diabetes distress (DDS or PAID), disordered eating screen, SDoH screen, vaccination review, DSMES reinforcement; cognitive screen + function / falls / frailty (≥65); malnutrition screen (weight-loss pharmacotherapy or post-metabolic surgery) — MNT (Medicare NCD 180.1: initial 3 hr + 2 hr / yr); DSMES at diagnosis, annually, with new complications, at care transitions (Medicare DSMT: 10 hr initial + 2 hr / yr)

Summary recommendation

Primary-care monitoring of an adult with Type 2 Diabetes is built around visits every 3–6 months — quarterly when not at glycemic goal or after a therapy change, every 6 months when stable and at goal.1 At every visit document weight / BMI, blood pressure (target <130/80; consider <120 systolic in high CV / kidney risk), hypoglycemia events, medication adherence and tolerability, tobacco use, and (when on CGM) time-in-range (target >70%).1 The annual lab panel includes HbA1c (twice yearly minimum; target <7% for most nonpregnant adults), UACR, serum creatinine / eGFR, lipid panel (annually, on or off statin)1, ALT / AST with FIB-4 for MASLD, and TSH periodically; B12 is checked periodically on long-term metformin and potassium when on ACEi / ARB / MRA / finerenone.12 Complications surveillance comprises annual comprehensive foot exam (every visit if prior ulcer or sensory loss), distal polyneuropathy assessment, dilated eye exam at diagnosis and every 1–2 years thereafter if low risk, and CKD risk-tier-driven kidney monitoring (1–4× yearly).1 Cardiovascular surveillance includes periodic ASCVD risk estimation (PREVENT)9, lipid optimization (LDL <70 primary prevention, <55 with ASCVD), and BP control. Psychosocial and geriatric surveillance — at least annual depression, diabetes distress, anxiety, disordered eating, SDoH, and (≥65) cognitive and function / fall-risk screening — round out the visit. Immunizations are reviewed annually; DSMES and MNT are reinforced at diagnosis, yearly, with new complications, or at care transitions.

Laboratory

  • HbA1c — every 6 months if at goal and stable; quarterly if not at goal or after therapy change. Target <7% for most nonpregnant adults; individualized (older adults / AGS: 7.5–8% typical).15
  • CGM — recommended from diagnosis for people on insulin or noninsulin hypoglycemia-causing therapy (Rec 7.15); recommended for older adults with T2D on insulin (13.5); consider for T2D on basal insulin. Review at each visit: TIR >70% and TBR <70 mg/dL <4% for most adults; older adults with complex / intermediate health TIR ≥50% and TBR <1%; TBR <54 mg/dL <1%.1
  • Lipid panel — at diagnosis / initial evaluation, then annually if not on lipid-lowering therapy (Rec 10.16); at LLT initiation, 4–12 weeks after initiation or dose change, and annually thereafter (10.17). Targets: LDL <70 mg/dL primary prevention age 40–75 at higher risk; LDL <55 mg/dL with ASCVD (and ≥50% reduction).1
  • Serum creatinine / eGFR — annually if eGFR ≥60 and normal albuminuria; risk-tier-driven if CKD present (G3 every 6–12 months; G4 every 3–5 months; G5 every 1–3 months).12
  • Urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio (UACR) — annually at minimum; 1–4× yearly per CKD heat-map risk category; 2 of 3 abnormal over 3–6 months to confirm. Bands: <30 normal, 30–299 moderately increased, ≥300 severely increased.12
  • Serum K+ / eGFR — at initiation and periodically on ACEi / ARB / steroidal MRA (Rec 10.11 / 11.6b); nonsteroidal MRA (finerenone; eGFR ≥25): K+ 1 month after initiation, then periodically (11.8); diuretics: hypokalemia check at routine visits and 7–14 days after initiation or dose change.1
  • TSH — at diagnosis and as clinically indicated.1
  • ALT / AST with FIB-4 (MASLD screen) — at diagnosis and annually; FIB-4 <1.3 low risk; FIB-4 >2.67 → refer to liver specialist. Prefer a GLP-1 RA with MASH benefit when at high fibrosis risk.1
  • Vitamin B12 — periodically on metformin >5 years; sooner with anemia or neuropathy.1

Imaging

  • DXA bone density — every 2–3 years in adults ≥65 with diabetes and in younger adults with diabetes plus multiple fracture risk factors (Rec 4.9). In T2D interpret T-score ≤−2.0 as equivalent to −2.5; consider osteoporosis drug therapy at T-score ≤−2.5, or −2.0 to −2.5 with added fracture risk (4.13a/b). Avoid TZDs and sulfonylureas when fracture risk is high.1

Physical examination

  • Blood pressure — every routine visit, or at least every 6 months (Rec 10.1); counsel all with HTN on home BP monitoring (10.2). Target <130/80 if safely attainable; SBP <120 should be encouraged in high CV or kidney risk (10.4). Older adults: <130/80 if safe; <140/90 for poor health or limited life expectancy (13.9).1
  • Weight, height, BMI, waist circumference — every routine visit.1
  • Comprehensive foot exam (inspection, deformity, pulses; 10-g monofilament or Ipswich touch test + one of pinprick / temperature / vibration) — at least annually; every visit if prior ulcer / amputation / sensory loss.1
  • PAD screen — at initial evaluation: lower-extremity pulses, capillary refill, dependent rubor, elevation pallor, venous filling time. Refer for ABI with toe pressures if claudication, rest pain, or decreased / absent pedal pulses.1
  • Dilated comprehensive eye exam (or validated retinal imaging) — at diagnosis; every 1–2 years if no retinopathy and at glycemic goal; annually if retinopathy; more often if progressing.1
  • Distal symmetric polyneuropathy assessment — annually starting at diagnosis.1
  • Dental / oral examination — at least annually.1

Functional / surveys

  • 10-yr ASCVD risk (PREVENT-ASCVD, ages 30–79) — periodically, per the 2026 ACC/AHA dyslipidemia guideline (ADA co-issuing society); ADA 2026 Section 10 assigns statin intensity by age and risk factors without naming a calculator.91
  • Hypoglycemia risk and event review — every visit on insulin or sulfonylurea. Target no level 2 / 3 events.1
  • Cognitive screening (Mini-Cog, MoCA) — annually for adults ≥65.15
  • Functional status / fall risk / frailty — annually for adults ≥65.15
  • Depression screen (PHQ-2 / PHQ-9) — at least annually.1
  • Diabetes distress (DDS or PAID) — at least annually.1
  • Anxiety (GAD-7) — at least annually (Rec 5.46). Fear of hypoglycemia — at least annually in high-risk or severe-hypoglycemia patients (5.47). If distress is not addressed at the visit, refer to behavioral health (5.45).1
  • Disordered eating screen — at least annually.1
  • Sleep health screen — routinely (new 2026 Rec 5.56).1
  • Smoking / tobacco / e-cigarette assessment + cessation counseling — every visit.1
  • Malnutrition screen — periodically on weight-loss pharmacotherapy or after metabolic surgery (new 2025 recommendation).1
  • Vaccination review — annually per CDC schedule / ADA Table 4.3: influenza annually; pneumococcal conjugate (PCV15/20/21) all adults ≥50 and younger with risk conditions; HBV if <60 (≥60 at clinician discretion); RZV ≥50; Tdap/Td; RSV single dose ≥75 and 50–74 at increased risk; COVID-19 by individual-based (shared clinical) decision-making.17
  • DSMES — at diagnosis, annually, with new complications, and at care transitions. CMS DSMT benefit: 10 hours initial + 2 hours / year follow-up.16
  • Medical Nutrition Therapy (RD-delivered) — initial 3 hours + 2 hours / year follow-up under Medicare NCD 180.1.61
  • Social determinants of health screen — routinely.1

Notes

  • A1c target disagreement. ADA: <7% for most nonpregnant adults; <6.5% may be appropriate with good health and low hypoglycemia risk (Rec 6.4). AACE: ≤6.5% when safely achievable (2023 figure; confirm in 2026 algorithm). AGS: 7.5–8% for most older adults, with explicit caution against <6.5% in this group.145
  • Older-adult target stratification. ADA Section 13: <7.0–7.5% (healthy older), <8.0% (intermediate health), avoid stringent targets (poor health). AGS sets a higher default regardless of health status.15
  • Albuminuria cadence in established DKD. KDIGO 2022 emphasizes risk-tier-driven monitoring (up to every 1–3 months in red zones) and confirmation with 2 of 3 abnormal specimens; ADA aligns but allows simpler annual monitoring in stable patients with normal eGFR and UACR. A KDIGO 2026 Diabetes-and-CKD focused-update draft (public review closed Apr 2026) is under revision; the 2022 guideline remains current.221
  • Screening age. USPSTF (2021): adults 35–70 with overweight / obesity every 3 years. ADA: universal screening at age ≥35 (earlier with risk factors) regardless of BMI.31
  • BP target. ADA 2026: <130/80 if safely attainable; SBP <120 should be encouraged in high CV / kidney risk (Rec 10.4). AHA/ACC 2025: <130/80 for all adults.18

Cataract

ICD-10: H25.x (age-related), H26.x (other / secondary) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 26.4% — rank 6 of 30 Category: Ophthalmic Primary associations consulted: AAO, USPSTF, NEI, CMS

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — — — — — —
Imaging — — — — — Optical biometry (axial length, keratometry, anterior chamber depth) — once preoperatively for IOL calculation
Physical exam — — — Comprehensive medical eye evaluation every 1–2 yr (age ≥65); at least yearly if comorbid diabetes regardless of cataract status Comprehensive medical eye evaluation (age 55–64, asymptomatic) — every 1–3 yr Post-op exam day 1 (or within 24–48 hr); post-op week 1–2; post-op month 1
Functional / surveys — — — Best-corrected visual acuity (BCVA) + refraction, IOP, slit-lamp at each comprehensive exam — Functional impairment assessment (VF-14 or ADL-specific: reading, driving, TV, vocational / recreational tasks) for surgical-referral documentation

Summary recommendation

A patient with a diagnosed non-surgical cataract should receive a comprehensive dilated medical eye evaluation every 1–2 years if age ≥65 (every 1–3 years if 55–64), per AAO Preferred Practice Pattern and NEI guidance; comorbid diabetes mandates annual dilated exams.214 At each visit BCVA with refraction, IOP, and slit-lamp biomicroscopy of the lens, anterior segment, and dilated fundus are standard.1 Surgical referral is not driven by a fixed visual-acuity threshold but by patient-specific functional impairment — inability to perform ADLs (reading, driving, TV, work, recreation) despite tolerable refractive correction — and is supported by Medicare LCDs and the AAO Cataract in the Adult Eye PPP.51 Standard post-operative follow-up is a three-visit cadence: day 1 (or within 24–48 hours for low-risk cases), week 1–2, and month 1 — checking VA, IOP, wound integrity, IOL position, and inflammation. PCPs should re-refer when patients report new or worsening functional vision impairment, post-operative symptoms (pain, vision loss, redness, photophobia), or have lapsed the 1–2 year interval.1

Laboratory

No laboratory monitoring is recommended for cataract surveillance.

Imaging

  • Optical biometry (axial length, keratometry, anterior chamber depth) — once preoperatively for IOL power calculation. Documented IOL calculation must be signed and dated in the chart per MAC requirements. L34413 (Palmetto, R17, eff. 2024-11-21) sets no fixed preoperative window; it requires a current best-corrected Snellen VA (distance or near) and patient-specific functional-impairment documentation not correctable by a tolerable change in glasses or contact lenses.15

Physical examination

  • Comprehensive medical eye evaluation (history, VA with pinhole acuity when reduced, external exam, pupils, motility, IOP, gonioscopy when angle-closure risk, slit-lamp biomicroscopy, dilated lens / fundus exam) — every 1–2 years if age ≥65; every 1–3 years if 55–64 (asymptomatic, no risk factors); comorbid diabetes: type 2 at diagnosis then at least yearly, type 1 annually beginning 5 yr after diagnosis, regardless of cataract status.2
  • Slit-lamp biomicroscopy (cornea, anterior chamber, iris, lens, vitreous, macula, peripheral retina, optic nerve) — at preoperative evaluation and at each routine eye exam; required preoperatively to confirm cataract as the etiology of visual loss.1
  • Intraocular pressure (tonometry) — at every comprehensive eye exam and at each post-operative visit.21
  • Postoperative day 1 (or within 24–48 hr) — VA, IOP, slit-lamp, wound integrity (Seidel), IOL position, anterior-chamber inflammation. Day 1 for complicated / high-risk cases; within 48 hours acceptable for uncomplicated low-risk.1
  • Postoperative week 1–2 — routine recovery check.1
  • Postoperative month 1 — final refraction, BCVA, IOP, slit-lamp, fundus exam; expect stable refraction, no inflammation, normal IOP.1

Functional / surveys

  • Best-corrected visual acuity (BCVA) with refraction — at each comprehensive eye exam; required preoperatively (most MACs within 3 months of surgery).15
  • Functional impairment assessment (VF-14, VF-8R, or patient-specific ADL impact: reading, driving, TV, vocational / recreational tasks; glare and contrast complaints) — at the preoperative evaluation. Documentation must be patient-specific (not cloned).15

Notes

  • USPSTF vs. AAO disagreement on venue. USPSTF (2022, Grade I — insufficient evidence) addresses primary-care visual-acuity screening in asymptomatic older adults; AAO PPP and NEI recommend routine comprehensive eye exams by eye-care professionals. The disagreement is about venue, not the value of eye exams in older adults.324
  • CMS coverage policies vary by MAC. Most MACs reject a fixed visual-acuity cutoff for cataract surgery; one (First Coast) documents 20/40 as a threshold. AAO supports functional-impairment documentation rather than a numeric VA threshold.51
  • Post-operative cadence. AAO PPP supports a three-visit cadence (day 1 or 24–48 hr; week 1–2; month 1) for routine cases; complicated cases require day 1.1

Footnotes

Ophthalmology. 2026;133(4):P202–P236. doi:10.1016/j.ophtha.2025.12.031. https://www.aao.org/education/preferred-practice-pattern/comprehensive-adult-medical-eye-evaluation-ppp See bibliography.md → aao-eye-eval-2026. ↩↩↩↩

  3. USPSTF. Screening for Impaired Visual Acuity in Older Adults: Recommendation Statement. JAMA. 2022;327(21):2123-2128 (Grade I). See bibliography.md → uspstf-vision-2022. ↩

Ischemic Heart Disease

ICD-10: I20–I25 (chronic coronary disease spectrum; stable angina, prior MI, post-revascularization) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 21.8% — rank 7 of 30 Category: Cardiovascular Primary associations consulted: ACC/AHA (2023 CCD Guideline; 2026 Dyslipidemia; 2025 ACS), USPSTF, CMS, CDC, NHLBI

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — Lipid panel during titration (4–12 wk post statin start / change) — Lipid panel (stable), HbA1c (if DM), influenza + COVID-19 vaccination review; RSV once (≥75, or 50–74 with CAD/HF) — Lp(a) once in adulthood if never measured
Imaging — — — — — Stress imaging (SPECT / PET / CMR / stress echo), coronary CTA, echocardiogram, or invasive angiography — only with new / worsening symptoms; routine surveillance is Class 3 (No Benefit)
Physical exam BP, weight / BMI, physical activity assessment — — — — —
Functional / surveys Adherence review (aspirin 75–100 mg or DAPT through indicated window; high-intensity statin; β-blocker if indicated; ACEi / ARB if indicated; SGLT2i / GLP-1 RA in eligible); tobacco use + cessation counseling — — PHQ-2 / PHQ-9 depression screen — Cardiac rehabilitation referral after MI (≤12 mo), PCI, CABG, stable angina, valve repair / replacement, transplant, or stable HFrEF ≤35% (36 sessions / 36 wk; ICR 72 sessions / 18 wk; 42 CFR 410.49)

Summary recommendation

For a primary-care patient with clinically stable chronic coronary disease (CCD), the 2023 ACC/AHA CCD Guideline frames surveillance around guideline-directed medical therapy, lifestyle support, and risk-factor monitoring rather than routine repeat cardiac imaging.1 Outpatient visits are individualized — commonly every 4–6 months and at least annually once stable — and at each visit clinicians should measure BP (target <130/80 mm Hg), weight and physical activity, assess tobacco use, screen for depression with PHQ-2 / PHQ-9, and verify adherence to aspirin 75–100 mg (or DAPT through the post-PCI / ACS window), high-intensity statin, ACEi / ARB if indicated, and SGLT2i / GLP-1 RA in eligible patients.1 A lipid panel is checked 4–12 weeks after any statin change and then every 3–12 months, targeting ≥50% LDL-C reduction; the 2026 ACC/AHA Dyslipidemia Guideline adds an LDL-C <55 mg/dL secondary-prevention goal, with escalation to ezetimibe, bempedoic acid, or a PCSK9 inhibitor when unmet.2 Cardiac rehabilitation should be referred after MI (within 12 months), PCI, CABG, stable angina, valve surgery, transplant, or stable HFrEF (LVEF ≤35%) — Medicare covers up to 36 sessions over up to 36 weeks (intensive CR up to 72 sessions over 18 weeks; 42 CFR 410.49)3.3 Routine surveillance stress testing, coronary CTA, echocardiography, or angiography is not recommended in asymptomatic stable patients (Class 3); escalate to stress imaging or angiography only when symptoms or functional capacity change despite optimized medical therapy.1

Laboratory

  • Fasting lipid panel — 4–12 weeks after statin initiation or dose change, then every 3–12 months. Target ≥50% LDL-C reduction AND LDL-C <70 mg/dL / non-HDL-C <100 mg/dL for all clinical ASCVD; LDL-C <55 mg/dL / non-HDL-C <85 mg/dL if very high risk (document rationale). Add nonstatin therapy if not at goal on maximally tolerated statin. ApoB optional to assess residual risk once LDL-C / non-HDL-C goals are met (esp. TG >200 mg/dL, diabetes, LDL-C <70).12
  • HbA1c (if diabetic) — per diabetes monitoring cadence. SGLT2i is Class I in CCD + HFrEF (LVEF ≤40%); GLP-1 RA / SGLT2i indicated for residual cardiometabolic risk.1
  • Lipoprotein(a) — once in adulthood if never measured; repeat generally not needed. If ≥125 nmol/L (≥50 mg/dL), intensify control of all risk factors and recommend cascade family screening.2
  • Annual influenza vaccination and current-season (2026–2027) COVID-19 vaccination — review annually. RSV vaccine — single lifetime dose (not annual) for all adults ≥75 and adults 50–74 with chronic heart disease (CAD, HF; isolated HTN does not qualify); do not repeat if already given.145

Imaging

  • Stress imaging (SPECT, PET, CMR, stress echo), coronary CTA, or invasive angiography — as indicated only when symptoms or functional status change despite optimized medical therapy. Routine periodic surveillance imaging is NOT recommended (Class 3, No Benefit).1
  • Echocardiogram (LVEF reassessment) — as indicated by change in clinical status (new HF symptoms / signs); not routine. Identifies LVEF ≤40% for GDMT including SGLT2i.1

Physical examination

  • Blood pressure — every visit. Target <130/80 mm Hg.1
  • Weight / BMI and physical activity assessment — every visit. Routine aerobic activity supported; ~1,000 additional steps/day associated with ~10% mortality reduction.1

Functional / surveys

  • Adherence review — aspirin 75–100 mg daily (or DAPT through indicated post-PCI / ACS window); high-intensity statin; β-blocker only if indicated (post-MI <1 year, LVEF ≤50%, or other indication); ACEi / ARB if indicated; SGLT2i / GLP-1 RA in eligible patients.1
  • Tobacco use and cessation counseling — every visit. Behavioral + pharmacotherapy first-line; not e-cigarettes.1
  • Depression screen (PHQ-2 → PHQ-9 if positive) — periodically.1
  • Cardiac rehabilitation referral — MI within preceding 12 mo, PCI / coronary stent, CABG, stable angina, heart-valve repair / replacement, heart or heart-lung transplant, or stable chronic HF (LVEF ≤35%, NYHA II–IV despite ≥6 wk optimal therapy). Medicare Part B: up to 36 sessions over up to 36 weeks (max 2 one-hour sessions/day); additional 36 sessions with MAC approval. Intensive CR: up to 72 one-hour sessions over up to 18 weeks (42 CFR 410.49).13
  • DAPT (post-ACS) — default 12 months (ticagrelor or prasugrel preferred P2Y12). After PCI, in patients who have tolerated DAPT with ticagrelor, transition to ticagrelor monotherapy at ≥1 month is recommended to reduce bleeding (Class 1, 2025 ACS). Shorter DAPT for high bleeding risk.6

Notes

  • Routine surveillance imaging is Class 3 (No Benefit). A significant shift from prior practice — the 2023 CCD Guideline removed routine periodic stress testing every 2–3 years from accepted surveillance.1
  • β-blocker duration post-MI. The 2023 CCD Guideline departed from long-standing tradition: long-term β-blocker beyond 1 year post-MI is not beneficial unless LVEF ≤50% or another indication is present (Class IIb against indefinite use).1
  • LDL secondary-prevention target. 2023 CCD emphasizes percent reduction (≥50%); 2026 ACC/AHA Dyslipidemia Guideline restores numeric goals: LDL-C <70 / non-HDL-C <100 mg/dL for all clinical ASCVD and LDL-C <55 / non-HDL-C <85 mg/dL for very-high-risk ASCVD, plus once-in-adulthood Lp(a). PREVENT equations do not apply to known ASCVD.12
  • DAPT duration. 2025 ACC/AHA ACS Guideline keeps 12-month DAPT as default but adds a Class 1 option of 1-month DAPT → ticagrelor monotherapy to reduce bleeding; it replaces the 2016 DAPT focused update.6
  • Depression screening cadence unspecified. CCD Guideline endorses screening without specifying an interval; USPSTF also endorses without defined interval.1

(NCD 20.10 retired; see Pub. 100-04, ch. 32, §140.) https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-42/chapter-IV/subchapter-B/part-410/subpart-B/section-410.49 See bibliography.md → cms-cr-cfr. ↩↩

Anemia

ICD-10: D50.x (iron-deficiency), D51.x (B12 deficiency), D52.x (folate deficiency), D53.x (other nutritional), D63.x (anemia in chronic disease) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 21.7% — rank 8 of 30 Category: Metabolic Primary associations consulted: KDIGO, ASH, AGA, AAFP, CDC, NIDDK, USPSTF

Anemia is a finding common to many underlying conditions; surveillance is subtype-specific. The sections below cover the dominant primary-care subtypes: iron deficiency anemia (IDA), anemia of chronic kidney disease, B12 / folate deficiency, and anemia in older adults / chronic disease.

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — CBC + retic + ferritin + TSAT q3 mo (CKD G5 / G5D); ferritin + TSAT + Hb at least q3 mo on iron / ESA in CKD (q1–3 mo on IV iron in HD); CBC q3 mo during first year of oral iron replacement CBC + retic + ferritin + TSAT (CKD G4) CBC + retic + ferritin + TSAT (CKD G3); follow-up CBC after iron repletion CBC every 5–10 yr in women of childbearing age (CDC) CBC + reticulocyte count, ferritin, B12, folate, eGFR, LDH / haptoglobin / bilirubin (workup); Hgb q2–4 wk on ESA initiation; CBC 1 mo after starting oral iron; reticulocyte 1 wk after B12 replacement; MMA / homocysteine 1 wk to 3 mo (B12 response); CBC at 8 wk (B12 replacement); GI evaluation (EGD + colonoscopy) for adult IDA in men and postmenopausal women
Imaging — — — — — —
Physical exam — — — — — Neurologic exam (vibration, proprioception, gait, cognition) at diagnosis and follow-up for B12 deficiency with neurologic findings
Functional / surveys Symptom assessment (fatigue, dyspnea, exercise tolerance, pica, restless legs) during active treatment — — — — Transfusion — symptom-driven in CKD (Hb <7 stable inpatients; <8 with CVD / orthopedic surgery); minimize in transplant candidates

Summary recommendation

Primary-care surveillance of known anemia is subtype-specific. In iron deficiency anemia on oral iron, check a CBC at ~4 weeks (expect ≥1 g/dL Hgb rise), then every 3 months for one year, continue iron approximately 3 months after Hgb normalizes, and verify repleted stores with a follow-up ferritin.23 Persistent or recurrent IDA in adult men or postmenopausal women warrants GI evaluation (EGD + colonoscopy).36 In anemia of CKD, KDIGO 2026 anchors monitoring: screen with CBC, reticulocytes, ferritin, and TSAT at least annually in G3, twice yearly in G4, and every 3 months in G5 / G5D; on iron, recheck TSAT / ferritin / Hb at least every 3 months (every 1–3 months on IV iron in HD) and withhold iron at ferritin >700 ng/mL or TSAT ≥40%; once on an ESA or HIF-PHI, monitor Hgb every 2–4 weeks during initiation and every 3 months in maintenance, with iron studies (ferritin, TSAT) every 3 months on ESA, targeting Hgb 10–11.5 g/dL while avoiding both Hgb <9 and >11.5 g/dL.1 For B12 deficiency on replacement, confirm response with reticulocytosis at 1 week, MMA / homocysteine normalization at 1 week to 3 months, and CBC normalization by 8 weeks; serial neurologic exams track recovery, and routine repeat B12 testing is not recommended once replacement is established.4 Folate deficiency monitoring parallels B12 (CBC at 8 weeks). For older adults with anemia of chronic disease or unexplained anemia, periodic CBC, iron studies, B12, eGFR, and TSH reclassify subtype; hemolysis labs (LDH, haptoglobin, bilirubin, peripheral smear) are added when Hgb drops without bleeding.5

Laboratory — Iron Deficiency Anemia

  • CBC — at 1 month after starting oral iron (expect ≥1 g/dL Hgb rise); every 3 months for 1 year; then 12 months later.23
  • Serum ferritin — at end of iron therapy course and 6–8 weeks after IV iron infusion. Target ferritin ≥30 ng/mL (AGA suggests ≥50 ng/mL).35
  • Diagnostic ferritin threshold for iron deficiency (with or without anemia) — ASH 2026: ≤30 ng/mL for adults including menstruating and pregnant people; ≤50 ng/mL may be used in higher-risk groups (e.g., heavy menstrual bleeding); with inflammation (cancer, IBD, infection) use ferritin <100 ng/mL or TSAT <20%. AGA 2020 uses <45 ng/mL in the GI-evaluation context.73
  • GI evaluation (EGD + colonoscopy) — at IDA diagnosis in adult men and postmenopausal women; repeat if IDA recurs after repletion. ~62% of older adults have a GI source.365

Laboratory — Anemia of CKD

  • Anemia panel (CBC, reticulocytes, ferritin, TSAT) — at referral, when anemia suspected, and at least annually for CKD G3; twice yearly for G4; every 3 months for G5 and G5D. Anemia = Hb <13.0 g/dL (male) / <12.0 g/dL (female).1
  • Hemoglobin (on ESA or HIF-PHI) — every 2–4 weeks after initiation or dose adjustment; at least every 3 months in maintenance (with iron studies). ESA preferred over HIF-PHI; HIF-PHI only for ESA hyporesponsiveness / intolerance or impractical ESA use (avoid with active malignancy or recent CV / thrombotic event; not FDA-approved in non-dialysis CKD). Stop HIF-PHI after 3–4 months if insufficient response.1
  • Iron studies (ferritin, TSAT) + Hb — CKD not on HD and on iron: at least every 3 months; CKD G5HD on IV iron: every 1–3 months. Check more often with ESA / HIF-PHI start or increase, blood loss, hospitalization, or overshoot. Start iron: non-HD if ferritin <100 ng/mL with TSAT <40%, or ferritin 100–300 with TSAT <25%; G5HD if ferritin ≤500 and TSAT ≤30% (IV preferred). Withhold iron if ferritin >700 ng/mL or TSAT ≥40%; suspend during active infection. Switch oral → IV if insufficient effect after 1–3 months or poor tolerance.1
  • Hgb thresholds — ESA initiation: dialysis Hb ≤9–10 g/dL; non-dialysis individualized, generally Hb 8.5–10.0 g/dL (lower with CVD / thromboembolism / active malignancy; higher for transplant candidates or symptomatic). Target ≤11.5 g/dL, typically 10–11.5. Avoid Hb rise >~1 g/dL per 2 weeks (reduce dose 25–50%). Consider suspending ESA during stroke or thrombosis.1
  • Initial anemia workup — CBC, reticulocytes, ferritin, TSAT. If unrevealing: smear, haptoglobin, LDH, CRP, B12, folate, LFTs, SPEP / immunofixation, serum free light chains, urine Bence-Jones, TSH, FOBT, ± PTH. Ferritin <45 ng/mL or MCV <80 fL with unexplained iron deficiency → evaluate for blood loss (GI / urology / gynecology referral as indicated).1

Laboratory — B12 / Folate Deficiency

  • Reticulocyte count — 1 week after B12 replacement; expect reticulocytosis.4
  • Methylmalonic acid (MMA) and / or homocysteine — 1 week post-replacement to confirm response; or 3–6 months after starting oral therapy (borderline B12 200–350 pg/mL).45
  • CBC with MCV — 8 weeks after starting B12 replacement; expect normalization of Hgb, MCV, WBC, platelets.4
  • Serum B12 — not routinely retested after replacement initiated (British Society of Haematology, cited by AAFP); may repeat at ≥2 months for adherence / response.4

Laboratory — Hemolysis Workup (As Indicated)

  • LDH, indirect bilirubin, haptoglobin, peripheral smear — when hemolysis suspected (Hgb drop without bleeding, elevated reticulocytes).5

Laboratory — Screening / Surveillance (General)

  • CBC screening — every 5–10 years through childbearing years at routine exam (CDC 1998 recommendation for nonpregnant women).6
  • eGFR / serum creatinine — at anemia evaluation and at CKD-stage-appropriate intervals to identify CKD as cause.18

Physical examination

  • Neurologic exam (vibration, proprioception, gait, cognition) — at diagnosis and follow-up visits during 6 weeks–3 months of replacement for B12 deficiency with neurologic findings. Some deficits are irreversible.4

Functional / surveys

  • Symptom assessment (fatigue, dyspnea, exercise tolerance, pica, restless legs) — every visit during active treatment.12
  • Transfusion — in CKD, anemia-related signs / symptoms are the primary trigger, not a fixed Hb. Reasonable thresholds: Hb <7 g/dL in stable adult inpatients; <7.5 for cardiac surgery; <8 for orthopedic surgery or significant CVD. Minimize transfusion in transplant candidates (allosensitization).15

Notes

  • Ferritin diagnostic cutoff for iron deficiency. WHO / older CDC used ≤15 ng/mL. ASH 2026 (final; conditional recommendations) sets ≤30 ng/mL for adults (≤50 in high-risk groups; <100 or TSAT <20% with inflammation). AGA 2020 uses 45 ng/mL. Conflict flagged: specialty societies differ (30 vs 45 ng/mL); no federal standard.73
  • Iron ceiling in CKD. KDIGO 2026 withholds routine iron when ferritin >700 ng/mL or TSAT ≥40% (PIVOTAL-based proactive dosing in HD); initiation thresholds differ for HD (≤500 / ≤30%) vs non-HD (<100 with TSAT <40%, or 100–300 with TSAT <25%). A trial of iron may be considered at ferritin 500–1200 with low TSAT in refractory anemia. KDOQI 2013 caution against routinely exceeding 500 predates PIVOTAL. Conflict flagged.1
  • Hgb target in CKD. KDIGO 2026 (final, Jan 2026) retains the ≤11.5 g/dL ceiling (typical 10–11.5) and individualizes ESA initiation (dialysis ≤9–10; non-dialysis 8.5–10). ESA is preferred over HIF-PHI.1
  • Repeat B12 testing. British Society of Haematology recommends against routine retesting after replacement begins; other sources suggest retesting MMA / homocysteine at 3–6 months in malabsorption.4
  • USPSTF has no current recommendation for or against IDA screening in nonpregnant adults; the 2024 statement on pregnancy is an "I" (insufficient evidence).9

Hypothyroidism

ICD-10: E03.x (primary hypothyroidism; includes congenital, autoimmune, post-treatment) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 21.3% — rank 9 of 30 Category: Metabolic Primary associations consulted: ATA, AACE, USPSTF, NIDDK

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — TSH every 4–8 wk during titration / dose change / formulation switch TSH at 6 mo after stabilization (then transition to annual); TSH q6–12 mo while observing subclinical hypothyroidism TSH (stable on levothyroxine); fasting lipid panel (untreated subclinical hypothyroidism) — Free T4 (dose adjustment, suspected non-adherence, central hypothyroidism, pregnancy); TPO antibodies once at subclinical-hypothyroidism workup; TSH q4 wk through ~20 wk gestation, then once between 26–32 wk; confirmatory repeat TSH at 2–3 mo for newly abnormal value before initiating therapy
Imaging — — — — — —
Physical exam Weight, heart rate, blood pressure (each visit during titration); at least annually once stable — — — — —
Functional / surveys Symptom assessment — residual hypothyroidism + signs of overtreatment (tachycardia, weight loss, tremor) — — — — —

Summary recommendation

An adult with stable primary hypothyroidism on levothyroxine should have TSH checked 4–8 weeks after any dose change or formulation switch, again at approximately 6 months once stable, and annually thereafter — or sooner with new symptoms, pregnancy, weight change >10%, or addition of medications that affect absorption (PPI, calcium, iron, estrogen).1 The TSH target is 0.4–4.0 mIU/L for most adults, with an age-adjusted upward shift to 4–6 mIU/L in patients aged 70–80 and tolerance up to ~7 mIU/L in those ≥80 to avoid iatrogenic thyrotoxicosis, atrial fibrillation, and fracture.12 Free T4 is not required at every visit but is checked during titration, in suspected non-adherence, in central hypothyroidism, and routinely in pregnancy. Pregnancy demands immediate dose increase of ~20–30% at confirmation, TSH every 4 weeks through midgestation with a target below ~4.0 mIU/L (ideally between the lower reference limit and 2.5 mIU/L when treated), and reversion to pre-pregnancy dose postpartum with rechecking at 6 weeks.3 For untreated subclinical hypothyroidism, repeat TSH (with free T4 and TPO antibody) at 2–3 months to confirm persistence, then monitor every 6–12 months; check a fasting lipid panel and weigh treatment more strongly when TSH is >10 mIU/L, TPO Ab positive, or cardiovascular risk elevated.2 Each visit should include weight, heart rate, blood pressure, and a symptom review for both residual hypothyroidism and signs of overtreatment.

Laboratory

  • Serum TSH (titration) — every 4–8 weeks during dose change, formulation switch, or initial titration.1
  • Serum TSH (stable maintenance) — at 6 months after stabilization, then annually. Target 0.4–4.0 mIU/L general adult; 4–6 mIU/L acceptable age 70–80; up to ~7 mIU/L acceptable for those ≥80.12
  • Serum TSH (pregnancy) — every 4 weeks through ~20 weeks gestation, then at least once between 26 and 32 weeks. Target: trimester-specific reference or below ~4.0 mIU/L if no local range; aim 0.4–2.5 mIU/L when on replacement.3
  • Serum TSH (subclinical hypothyroidism, observed) — every 6–12 months while observed; sooner if symptoms develop.2
  • Confirmatory repeat TSH — 2–3 months after any newly abnormal TSH before initiating therapy.52
  • Free T4 — as indicated; routinely co-measured during dose titration, in suspected non-adherence, central hypothyroidism, and pregnancy. Not required at every follow-up in stable primary hypothyroidism.13
  • Free or total T3 — not routinely recommended; only when combination LT4 / LT3 therapy is used or clinical concern.14
  • TPO antibodies (anti-thyroid peroxidase) — once at diagnosis of subclinical hypothyroidism or unexplained TSH elevation; predicts progression. Pregnancy workup if TSH >2.5 mIU/L.23
  • Fasting lipid panel — at baseline in untreated subclinical hypothyroidism and after treatment normalization; treatment of subclinical hypothyroidism may improve lipids.2

Imaging

No routine imaging is recommended for surveillance of treated primary hypothyroidism.

Physical examination

  • Weight, heart rate, blood pressure — each visit during dose titration; at least annually once stable. Watch for iatrogenic thyrotoxicosis (tachycardia, weight loss, tremor).1

Functional / surveys

  • Symptom assessment (fatigue, cold intolerance, constipation, mood, cognition; and hyperthyroid symptoms suggesting overtreatment) — every visit.12

Notes

  • USPSTF vs. specialty societies on screening. USPSTF 2015 finds insufficient evidence (Grade I) to screen nonpregnant asymptomatic adults; AACE / ATA 2012 endorses aggressive case-finding in higher-risk adults (pregnancy planning, age >60, autoimmune disease, prior neck irradiation).52
  • Subclinical hypothyroidism treatment threshold. AACE / ATA 2012 supports considering treatment for TSH 4.5–10 mIU/L when symptomatic, TPO Ab positive, or with CV disease. TRUST trial and ETA-influenced commentary argue against treating older adults with TSH ≤10 mIU/L.2
  • Combination LT4 / LT3. ATA 2014 recommends against routine use (strong). The 2021 ATA / BTA / ETA Joint Consensus softened this, allowing a trial in selected symptomatic patients with biochemical euthyroidism, while reaffirming LT4 monotherapy as standard.14
  • TSH upper limit by age. ATA endorses an age-shifted target in older adults (4–6 mIU/L at 70–80; up to ~7 at ≥80). Some laboratory references still apply a uniform 4.5 mIU/L cutoff.1
  • Pregnancy guidance. ATA 2017 pregnancy guideline superseded by ATA 2026 (Preconception, Pregnancy, and Postpartum; Thyroid 2026;36(5)). Pregnancy-specific TSH targets and monitoring intervals in this monograph are carried over from 2017 pending verification against the 2026 full text. The 2026 update reportedly no longer uses TPOAb status to guide LT4 in subclinical hypothyroidism in pregnancy.3

  2. Garber JR et al. Clinical Practice Guidelines for Hypothyroidism in Adults — AACE / ATA. Endocr Pract. 2012;18(6):988-1028. See bibliography.md → aace-ata-hypo-2012. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

  3. Korevaar TIM, Leung AM, Alexander EK, et al. American Thyroid Association 2026 Guidelines for Thyroid Disease in Preconception, Pregnancy, and Postpartum. Thyroid. 2026;36(5):481–544. doi:10.1177/10507256261445624. https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1177/10507256261445624 See bibliography.md → ata-preg-2026. ↩↩↩↩↩

  4. Jonklaas J et al. Evidence-Based Use of Levothyroxine / Liothyronine Combinations — ATA / BTA / ETA Consensus. Thyroid. 2021;31(2):156-182. See bibliography.md → ata-bta-eta-combo-2021. ↩↩
