Depression / Bipolar / Mood Disorders

ICD-10: F32.x (MDD single episode), F33.x (MDD recurrent), F31.x (bipolar disorder), F34.x (persistent mood) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 19.7% — rank 10 of 30 Category: Mental Health Primary associations consulted: USPSTF, APA, VA/DoD, AGS, CMS, SAMHSA, ADA/APA Consensus

CCW groups depression, bipolar disorder, and other mood disorders into a single category. Depression is the dominant component; bipolar-specific surveillance and medication-monitoring sections are flagged below.

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — Lithium level q3 mo (stable); valproate or carbamazepine level + CBC + LFTs q3–6 mo (stable); weight on SGA quarterly after first 12 wk Lithium Cr / eGFR, BUN, Na, K, Ca, TSH, PTH q6–12 mo (stable); CBC + LFTs (valproate / carbamazepine stable) BP + fasting glucose + lipids (SGA users, annually after the 12-wk visit) — Lithium level twice weekly during initiation, within 5–7 d of dose change; carbamazepine CBC + LFTs q2–4 wk first 2 mo; weight on SGA at baseline / 4 / 8 / 12 wk
Imaging — — — — — —
Physical exam Weight / BMI (SGA users — baseline, 4, 8, 12 wk, then quarterly); BP (SGA users) AIMS q6 mo (q3 mo high-risk) on antipsychotic therapy — BP + AIMS annually (SGA stable) — —
Functional / surveys Adherence + adverse-effect review; suicide-risk reassessment (C-SSRS "Since Last Visit" or PHQ-9 item 9 follow-up) during active treatment PHQ-9 monthly during acute treatment; every 2–4 wk in continuation; periodically in maintenance / recovery; GDS-15 or PHQ-9 in older adults; PHQ-9 / GDS q6 mo in nursing-home residents (2–4 wk post-admission, then q6 mo) — PHQ-2 / PHQ-9 screening per USPSTF 2023 for previously unscreened adults — MDQ at initial depression workup (rule out bipolar); CoCM / BHI registry tracking

Summary recommendation

Primary-care monitoring of diagnosed depression follows a measurement-based-care framework: administer the PHQ-9 at baseline and at least monthly during acute treatment (weekly to biweekly in many systems), every 2–4 weeks during continuation, and periodically through maintenance, targeting response (≥50% PHQ-9 reduction) and remission (PHQ-9 ≤4).12 Suicide risk is reassessed at every visit during active treatment using PHQ-9 item 9 plus a structured tool such as the Columbia Suicide Severity Rating Scale "Since Last Visit," with more frequent contact in the first 4–12 weeks after starting or changing an antidepressant.16 Older adults are tracked with the GDS-15 or PHQ-9; nursing-home residents are reassessed 2–4 weeks after admission and every 6 months.7 Medication-specific monitoring varies by agent: SSRIs / SNRIs need baseline metabolic and tolerability review with reassessment at 2 weeks and at dose changes; lithium requires 12-hour trough levels every 3 months when stable (target usually 0.6–0.8 mmol/L, <0.8 where possible; 0.4–0.8 at age 60–79, 0.4–0.7 at ≥80) with creatinine / eGFR, electrolytes, calcium, TSH, and PTH every 6–12 months in stable patients (more often early) and nephrology referral at eGFR <609; valproate and carbamazepine require periodic drug levels, CBC, and LFTs; second-generation antipsychotics used for bipolar or augmentation require ADA / APA metabolic monitoring of weight (baseline, 4 / 8 / 12 weeks, then quarterly), BP and fasting glucose and lipids (baseline, 12 weeks, then annually), and AIMS every 6 months.35 The MDQ should be administered when a new depressive episode is being evaluated to avoid missing bipolar disorder.3 Practices using the CMS Collaborative Care Model (CoCM, CPT 99492–99494) or Behavioral Health Integration (CPT 99484) embed all of this through a care-manager registry with monthly psychiatric caseload review.8

Laboratory — Mood-Stabilizer / Antipsychotic Monitoring

  • Serum lithium level (12-hour trough) — ~1 week after start (longer in older adults / low eGFR), then twice weekly to weekly until stable; within 5–7 days of any dose change; every 3 months in stable maintenance (IGSLi/ISBD 2026: monthly to 3-monthly; APA 2002/2005: 6–12 months; the more conservative interval is used). Maintenance usually 0.6–0.8 mmol/L (individualize; keep <0.8 where possible to limit CKD risk); older adults 0.4–0.8 mmol/L at age 60–79 and 0.4–0.7 mmol/L at ≥80.39
  • Serum creatinine / eGFR + BUN, sodium, potassium, calcium (lithium) — baseline; every 2–3 months for first 6 months; every 6–12 months stable (more often if older, reduced or rapidly declining eGFR, comorbidity, or after intoxication). Investigate rises >25%. Nephrology referral if eGFR <60; co-manage as eGFR approaches 30. Fall >5 mL/min/yr or eGFR ~30–40 favors tapering (taper over ≥3 months).39
  • TSH and calcium / PTH (lithium) — baseline; TSH once or twice in first 6 months; thyroid and parathyroid function every 6–12 months.39
  • Valproate level, CBC, LFTs — VPA level after titration and with dose changes; CBC and LFTs baseline, then every 6–12 months. Target VPA 50–125 mcg/mL.3
  • Carbamazepine level, CBC with platelets, LFTs, sodium — level with dose changes and every 6–12 months; CBC and LFTs every 2–4 weeks for first 2 months, then every 3–6 months. Target 4–12 mcg/mL.3
  • Fasting plasma glucose or HbA1c (SGA users) — baseline, 12 weeks, then annually. FPG target <100 mg/dL.5
  • Fasting lipid panel (SGA users) — baseline, 12 weeks, then every 5 years (every 2 years if abnormal). Many systems and APA bipolar guidance now favor annual.53

Physical examination

  • Weight / BMI (SGA users) — baseline, 4, 8, 12 weeks, then quarterly. Weight gain ≥5% triggers intervention.5
  • Blood pressure (SGA users) — baseline, 12 weeks, then annually (more often if elevated). Target <130/80 mm Hg.5
  • Abnormal Involuntary Movement Scale (AIMS) — baseline, then every 6 months (every 3 months for high-risk patients) on antipsychotic therapy. Detect tardive dyskinesia early.3

Functional / surveys

  • PHQ-9 (Patient Health Questionnaire-9) — baseline; at least monthly during acute treatment or any treatment change; every 2–4 weeks in continuation; periodically in maintenance / recovery. Response = ≥50% reduction from baseline; remission = total score ≤4 (some sources use <5).1
  • PHQ-9 or PHQ-2 (screening, USPSTF 2023) — at least once for previously unscreened adults including pregnant / postpartum and ≥65; clinician judgment for re-screening intervals. PHQ-2 ≥2 triggers PHQ-9; PHQ-9 ≥10 indicates probable MDD.4
  • Geriatric Depression Scale (GDS-15) or PHQ-9 — baseline and to monitor treatment response in adults ≥65; every 6 months in nursing-home residents (2–4 wk post-admission, then q6 mo). GDS-15 ≥5 positive.7
  • Columbia-Suicide Severity Rating Scale (C-SSRS "Since Last Visit") — at each visit during active treatment; whenever PHQ-9 item 9 is positive or new risk factors emerge.16
  • Mood Disorder Questionnaire (MDQ) — once at initial depression workup to rule out bipolar; repeat if hypomanic / manic features emerge. MDQ ≥7 with functional impairment suggests bipolar spectrum.3
  • Adherence and adverse-effect review — every visit during the acute phase; at every PHQ-9 administration.1
  • Collaborative Care Model registry / caseload review — continuous; care manager contacts and stepped review at least monthly until improvement, then maintenance contacts. Covered as CMS CoCM (CPT 99492 / 99493 / 99494) or general BHI (99484).8

Notes

  • Screening vs. ongoing monitoring. USPSTF 2023 addresses only undiagnosed adults and states evidence is insufficient on optimal re-screening intervals; APA and VA/DoD pick up at diagnosis and prescribe systematic measurement-based care. No federal recommendation exists on PHQ-9 cadence in already-diagnosed patients.41
  • Universal suicide-risk screening. USPSTF 2023 issued an "I" (insufficient) statement; VA/DoD, APA, and Joint Commission NPSG 15.01.01 require structured suicide-risk assessment at every visit for patients in active depression treatment.41
  • Lithium monitoring interval. 2002 / 2005 APA bipolar guideline (now APA 'legacy') suggests serum lithium every 6–12 months in stable patients. 2026 IGSLi/ISBD renal expert opinion: levels monthly to 3-monthly after titration, renal panel + electrolytes + calcium every 6–12 months, nephrology referral at eGFR <60, and keep levels <0.8 mmol/L (CKD risk rises at ≥0.6–0.8); older adults 0.4–0.8 (60–79) / 0.4–0.7 (≥80). Conflict flagged; the conservative interval (q3 mo) is adopted.39
  • SGA metabolic monitoring intensity. ADA / APA 2004 calls for fasting lipids every 5 years; subsequent guidance (APA bipolar, NCQA HEDIS SMD / SMC) effectively requires annual — a de facto stricter standard.5
  • PHQ-9 remission threshold. VA/DoD and HEDIS DRR-E define remission as PHQ-9 <5; some APA materials and Kroenke validation use ≤4. Clinically negligible but affects quality-measure reporting.1
  • APA guideline status. APA now lists its MDD (2010) and bipolar (2002) guidelines as 'Legacy'; no current APA guideline exists for either, and none is listed in development. VA/DoD MDD 2022 remains the most recent US federal MDD guideline.231

Chronic Kidney Disease

ICD-10: N18.x (CKD stages 1–5), N19 (unspecified renal failure) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 18.7% — rank 11 of 30 Category: Renal Primary associations consulted: KDIGO, NKF KDOQI, ACC/AHA, USPSTF (2012 statement inactive; update in progress), CDC, CMS, ASN, NIDDK

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs BMP (Na, K, HCO3, BUN, Cr / eGFR) on RAAS / SGLT2i / MRA or eGFR <30 Cr / eGFR + UACR for G4 (≈3×/yr), G3b A3, G5 (≈4×/yr); Ca / PO4 / PTH for G4 (q3–6 mo) and G5 (q1–3 mo); anemia panel (CBC, retics, ferritin, TSAT) for G5 non-dialysis; Hb + ferritin + TSAT on iron therapy Cr / eGFR + UACR for G3a A2, G3b A1–A2; anemia panel (CBC, retics, ferritin, TSAT) for G4; Ca / PO4 / PTH for G3a–G3b Cr / eGFR + UACR for G1–G2 A1–A2 and G3a A1; anemia panel (CBC, retics, ferritin, TSAT) for G3; HbA1c ≥2×/yr if diabetic; vaccination review (influenza, COVID-19, pneumococcal ≥50, RSV ≥75 or 50–74 at risk, HBV) — Cystatin C with eGFRcr-cys when clinical decision hinges on accuracy; urinalysis at diagnosis or change in status; lipid panel once at diagnosis (no routine repeat); K + Cr at 2–4 wk after starting / titrating ACEi / ARB / SGLT2i / MRA; iron studies if anemia evaluated; 25-OH vitamin D at baseline; alkaline phosphatase q12 mo G4–G5
Imaging — — — — — —
Physical exam Standardized office BP (5-min rest, validated cuff, multi-step protocol, average of readings); target SBP <120 mm Hg — — — — —
Functional / surveys Medication reconciliation (NSAID avoidance, contrast safety, dose adjustment); lifestyle counseling (Na <2 g/d, ≥150 min/wk activity, plant-rich diet, smoking cessation, weight) — — KFRE 2- and 5-year risk recalculation — Nephrology referral when 5-yr KFRE ≥3–5%, 2-yr ≥10%, eGFR <30, persistent A3 albuminuria, refractory hypertension / hyperkalemia / anemia / CKD-MBD, or unexplained decline; transplant / dialysis-access workup

Summary recommendation

Primary-care surveillance of established CKD is driven by the KDIGO 2024 heat map: combine current eGFR (race-free CKD-EPI 2021 creatinine equation, adding cystatin C when clinical decisions hinge on accuracy) with UACR, then plot on the G1–G5 / A1–A3 grid.1 Low-risk patients (G1–G2 A1) need annual eGFR + UACR; moderate-risk (e.g., G1–G2 A2, G3a A1) twice yearly; high-risk (G3a A2, G3b A1–A2, any G1–G2 A3) two to three times yearly; very-high-risk (G3b A3, all G4, all G5) three to four times yearly, with BP, electrolytes, and bicarbonate at each draw.1 At G3a add CKD-MBD labs — calcium, phosphate, PTH, alkaline phosphatase every 6–12 months, tightening to every 3–6 months at G4 and every 1–3 months at G5.4 Screen for anemia (CBC, reticulocytes, ferritin, TSAT) at least annually in G3, twice yearly in G4, and every 3 months in G5 (KDIGO 2026 Anemia in CKD).5 Check a lipid panel once at diagnosis for risk stratification, then "fire-and-forget" — start a statin (or statin / ezetimibe) in all adults ≥50 with eGFR <60 without routinely retesting LDL.6 In diabetics, HbA1c twice yearly (quarterly if unstable), individualized 6.5–8.0%, with SGLT2i (eGFR ≥20), GLP-1 RA for residual cardiometabolic risk, and non-steroidal MRA (finerenone) for persistent albuminuria with serum K <5.3 Target standardized office SBP <120 mm Hg.2 ACEi or ARB at maximum tolerated dose in A2–A3, continued even when eGFR falls <30 unless intolerable. Recheck K and creatinine 2–4 weeks after starting / titrating RAAS, SGLT2i, or MRA. Refer to nephrology when 5-year KFRE ≥3–5%, 2-year ≥10%, eGFR <30, persistent A3 albuminuria, or refractory complications.1

Laboratory

  • Serum creatinine with eGFR (CKD-EPI 2021 race-free) — risk-tier cadence per KDIGO 2024 heat map: G1A2 annual; G1A3 ~3×/yr; G3aA1 annual; G3aA2 2×/yr; G3bA1–A2 2×/yr; G3bA3 3×/yr; G4 any A ~3×/yr; G5 ~4×/yr. A change >20% triggers evaluation.1
  • Urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio (UACR), spot, first-morning preferred — same risk-tier cadence as eGFR. A1 <30 mg/g, A2 30–300, A3 >300. Doubling on repeat exceeds variability and warrants evaluation.1
  • Cystatin C with eGFRcr-cys — as indicated when creatinine-based estimate affects clinical decisions (drug dosing, referral, transplant eligibility).1
  • Serum electrolytes / BMP (Na, K, HCO3, BUN) — with each eGFR draw; more often on ACEi / ARB / SGLT2i / MRA or eGFR <30. Target HCO3 ≥22 mmol/L; K within normal range.1
  • Serum calcium and phosphate — G3a–G3b every 6–12 months; G4 every 3–6 months; G5 non-dialysis every 1–3 months.4
  • Intact PTH — G3a–G3b based on baseline and progression; G4 every 6–12 months; G5 non-dialysis every 3–6 months. No single non-dialysis CKD target; treat persistently rising or markedly elevated values.4
  • Alkaline phosphatase — every 12 months in G4–G5; more often if PTH elevated.4
  • 25-hydroxyvitamin D — at baseline; repeat based on level and intervention.4
  • Anemia panel (CBC, reticulocytes, ferritin, TSAT) — at referral; at least annually G3, at least twice yearly G4, at least every 3 months G5 / G5D; more often on ESA / HIF-PHI, Hb below target, or rapid CKD progression. Anemia = Hb <13.0 g/dL (men), <12.0 g/dL (women). On ESA: Hb every 2–4 weeks after start / dose change (avoid rise >1.0 g/dL per interval), at least every 3 months in maintenance; target Hb <11.5 g/dL (1D), individualized. On HIF-PHI: Hb 2–4 weeks after start / dose change, then every 4 weeks.5
  • Iron studies (ferritin, TSAT) — with each anemia panel; on iron therapy Hb + ferritin + TSAT every 3 months (non-dialysis / PD) or every 1–3 months (HD); more often after ESA / HIF-PHI start or dose increase, blood loss, or hospitalization. Treat iron deficiency (non-dialysis / PD) if ferritin <100 ng/mL and TSAT <40%, or ferritin 100–<300 ng/mL with TSAT <25%; hold routine iron if ferritin >700 ng/mL or TSAT ≥40%.5
  • Lipid panel — once at CKD diagnosis to assess 10-year ASCVD risk; routine follow-up lipid testing NOT recommended ("fire-and-forget"). Statin (or statin / ezetimibe) in adults ≥50 with eGFR <60 (G3a–G5 non-dialysis / non-transplant); statin in adults 18–49 with CAD / DM / prior CVA / 10-yr CHD risk >10%.6
  • HbA1c (if diabetic) — at least twice yearly meeting target and stable; quarterly if therapy changing or not at target. Individualized 6.5–8.0%; less reliable as eGFR <30.3
  • K + Cr at 2–4 weeks — after starting / titrating ACEi / ARB / SGLT2i / MRA. Continue ACEi / ARB even when eGFR falls <30 unless intolerable; tolerate creatinine rise up to 30% and initial eGFR dip up to 30% on SGLT2i.12
  • Urinalysis (dipstick + microscopy as indicated) — at diagnosis and as indicated for change in status, hematuria evaluation.1

Imaging

No routine imaging is recommended for surveillance.1

Physical examination

  • Standardized office blood pressure — every visit (5-min rest, validated cuff, multi-step protocol, average of readings). Target SBP <120 mm Hg when tolerated (KDIGO 2021).2

Functional / surveys

  • Kidney failure risk equation (KFRE), 2- and 5-year — annually for G3–G5; before referral decisions. Refer to nephrology when 5-yr KFRE ≥3–5% or 2-yr ≥10%.1
  • Medication reconciliation (NSAID avoidance, contrast safety, dose adjustment) — each visit.1
  • Lifestyle counseling (sodium <2 g/day, physical activity ≥150 min/wk, plant-rich diet, smoking cessation, weight) — each visit.12
  • Vaccination status (influenza annually; COVID-19 per current CDC season; PCV20 or PCV21 single dose for all ≥50 and younger adults with risk conditions; RSV single dose, not repeated, for all ≥75 and 50–74 at increased risk, e.g., dialysis / ESRD or diabetes with CKD; hepatitis B) — review annually. HBV serology if planning dialysis or transplant.1111213

Notes

  • BP target disagreement. KDIGO 2021 / 2024 endorse standardized-office SBP <120 mm Hg (Grade 2B). ACC/AHA 2025 (replaces 2017) keeps <130/80 for CKD; KDOQI 2026 commentary supports <130/80 while noting evidence for lower SBP in selected patients; ESC / ESH allows 130–139 systolic; NICE 2019 keeps <140/90. The KDIGO target is contingent on SPRINT-style measurement technique; the lower target is reflected here and the conflict is flagged.2910
  • Race-free eGFR uptake. KDIGO 2024 fully endorses the 2021 CKD-EPI race-free creatinine equation and broader cystatin C use; uptake in US labs has been uneven.1
  • Monitoring cadence. KDIGO 2024's risk-grid cadence is a practice point, not a graded recommendation; KDOQI 2024 US commentary supports it but notes lack of RCT data.1
  • Lipid follow-up paradigm difference. KDIGO 2013 / 2024 "fire-and-forget" omits routine LDL retesting; ACC/AHA endorses periodic monitoring for adherence and titration — different paradigms in the same patient.67
  • Hgb target in CKD anemia. KDIGO 2026 (final; replaces 2012) keeps ESA-treated Hb <11.5 g/dL (1D) with an individualized target; ESA initiation in non-dialysis CKD is symptom- and risk-based with no fixed Hb trigger; HIF-PHIs are avoided in people at increased risk of adverse events and are never combined with ESAs.5
  • USPSTF vs. specialty. USPSTF's 2012 CKD screening statement is listed as inactive / out of date, with an update in progress. KDIGO and NKF recommend opportunistic case-finding in diabetes, hypertension, age >60, CVD, and family history. The 2025 AHA/ACC BP guideline adds UACR testing for all patients with hypertension.819

Glaucoma

ICD-10: H40.x (glaucoma — POAG, NTG, angle-closure, suspect) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 15.0% — rank 12 of 30 Category: Ophthalmic Primary associations consulted: AAO, USPSTF, NEI, Glaucoma Research Foundation

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — — — — — —
Imaging — — OCT of peripapillary RNFL / optic nerve head / macular GCC (every 6–12 mo; complementary to visual field) OCT (stable, annual) — Stereo disc photography or imaging at baseline + as indicated
Physical exam Tonometry (IOP); medication adherence + ocular surface assessment Comprehensive eye exam (every 1–3 mo if progressing despite control; every 1–2 mo if progressing and uncontrolled) Comprehensive eye exam (every 3–6 mo if recently controlled) Comprehensive eye exam (every 6–12 mo if stable target IOP, no progression >6 mo); gonioscopy (every 1–5 yr or with change) — Central corneal thickness (pachymetry) at baseline; post-LPI / post-surgical follow-up
Functional / surveys — — Automated perimetry (Humphrey 24-2 or 30-2; every 6–12 mo); 10-2 for advanced disease Visual field (stable) — Up to 2×/year visual field in first 2 years post-diagnosis (≥6 tests in 2 yr to establish progression rate); target IOP setting + adjustment

Summary recommendation

USPSTF (2022, Grade I) finds insufficient evidence to recommend for or against routine primary-care screening for glaucoma in asymptomatic adults — but does not say "don't refer."3 NEI and AAO recommend a baseline comprehensive dilated eye exam at age 40 for all adults, and every 1–2 years for higher-risk groups (Black adults ≥40, all adults ≥60, Hispanic / Latino ≥60, family history of glaucoma, diabetes); PCPs refer these populations on that cadence and send any patient with elevated IOP, suspicious cup-to-disc ratio, or family history for a glaucoma-specific evaluation.41 Once a patient carries a diagnosis of POAG, NTG, or angle-closure glaucoma, ophthalmology owns surveillance per the AAO POAG PPP 2025 follow-up grid: every 6–12 months if target IOP achieved and stable >6 months; every 3–6 months if recently controlled; every 1–3 months if progressing despite control; and every 1–2 months if progressing and uncontrolled.1 Target IOP is individualized at 20–30% below untreated baseline, with stricter targets for advanced disease (also reflected in MIPS Measure #141, which requires 20% IOP reduction or documented plan of care).15 The PCP's continuing role is reinforcing drop adherence, reviewing systemic effects of topical β-blockers (bradycardia, bronchospasm) and prostaglandin analogs, flagging missed ophthalmology visits, and recognizing red flags — acute eye pain with halos / nausea (acute angle closure, emergent referral), rapid vision loss, or new field defect.

Laboratory

No laboratory monitoring is recommended for glaucoma surveillance.

Imaging

  • OCT of peripapillary RNFL, optic nerve head, and macular ganglion cell complex — at baseline and approximately every 6–12 months; complementary to visual field for progression detection (structural change often precedes detectable functional change in early disease).1
  • Stereo disc photography or imaging — at baseline and as indicated to document optic nerve appearance.1

Physical examination

  • Comprehensive medical eye evaluation (visual acuity, slit-lamp biomicroscopy, IOP, gonioscopy, dilated optic nerve / fundus exam) — cadence by control / progression status: every 6–12 mo if target IOP achieved and stable >6 mo; every 3–6 mo if recently controlled (≤6 mo); every 1–3 mo if progressing despite IOP control; every 1–2 mo if progressing and target IOP not achieved.1
  • Tonometry (Goldmann applanation preferred) — every visit. Target 20–30% reduction below untreated baseline (often <18 mm Hg moderate, <15 mm Hg advanced).1
  • Gonioscopy — at initial evaluation; repeat periodically (every 1–5 years, or when angle-closure component suspected, anterior chamber shallowing, or unexplained IOP change).12
  • Central corneal thickness (pachymetry) — at baseline; repeat after corneal surgery or unexplained IOP change.1
  • Dilated optic nerve / stereo disc evaluation — at each follow-up; document neuroretinal rim, cup-to-disc ratio, RNFL.1
  • Post–laser peripheral iridotomy (LPI) follow-up — within weeks after LPI, then long-term periodic surveillance every 6–12 months for progressive PAS and glaucomatous optic neuropathy.2
  • Post-surgical follow-up (trabeculectomy / tube shunt / MIGS) — early post-op per surgeon; long-term every 3–6 months minimum.1
  • Medication adherence and ocular surface assessment — every visit.1

Functional / surveys

  • Automated standard automated perimetry / visual field testing (Humphrey 24-2 or 30-2; 10-2 for advanced disease) — at diagnosis baseline; ≥6 tests in first 2 years to establish progression rate; then every 6–12 months; up to 2×/year for progressing eyes.1
  • Target IOP setting and documentation — at diagnosis; reassessed at each visit; adjust downward if progression occurs. MIPS Measure #141 requires 20% IOP reduction or documented plan of care.15
  • Population-level eye-exam cadence (primary-care referral) — baseline at age 40; every 1–2 years for high-risk groups (Black ≥40, all ≥60, Hispanic / Latino ≥60, family history, diabetes).41

Notes

  • USPSTF vs. AAO / NEI on venue. USPSTF (2022, I-statement) declines to endorse population screening in primary care; AAO and NEI recommend periodic comprehensive eye exams by an eye-care professional particularly for high-risk adults. The disagreement is about venue, not whether high-risk patients benefit from evaluation.34
  • OCT vs. visual field as progression metric. AAO POAG PPP 2025 calls them complementary; OCT often precedes functional change in early disease, while visual field is more informative in moderate–advanced disease where OCT "floors out."1
  • Gonioscopy cadence. PPP recommends gonioscopy at baseline and periodically without a fixed interval; real-world practice varies (every 1–5 years).1
  • Target IOP magnitude. AAO recommends 20–30% reduction below baseline; OHTS / EMGT data support ≥20% threshold; MIPS Measure #141 uses 20%. Advanced disease often requires >30% reduction, but no single universal target exists.15

Ophthalmology. 2026;133(4):P153–P201. doi:10.1016/j.ophtha.2025.12.030. https://scholars.duke.edu/publication/1705247 See bibliography.md → aao-angle-2026. ↩↩

Atrial Fibrillation and Flutter

ICD-10: I48.0–I48.92 (paroxysmal, persistent, chronic, atypical, typical flutter) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 14.1% — rank 13 of 30 Category: Cardiovascular Primary associations consulted: ACC/AHA/ACCP/HRS, USPSTF, CMS

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — INR on warfarin (at least monthly once stable; every 3–6 mo as a floor); BMP / Cr / eGFR / hepatic on DOAC at lower CrCl (≈every CrCl/10 months), age ≥75, frailty — Cr / eGFR (DOAC stable); HbA1c (if DM); lipid panel; CBC (Hgb) for occult bleeding — INR weekly during initiation / change / interacting drug or illness; Cr / hepatic / CBC for symptoms or bleeding
Imaging ECG / rhythm strip if symptom or rhythm change — — — — TTE at diagnosis; repeat with clinical change / new murmur / pre-procedure; TEE / cardiac CT pre-cardioversion (if <3 wk OAC) and pre-ablation / pre-LAAO; ambulatory rhythm monitoring (Holter / patch / event / ILR) post-ablation, symptom-driven
Physical exam BP, HR + rhythm assessment, weight / BMI — — — — —
Functional / surveys Symptoms (EHRA / CCS-SAF or palpitations / dyspnea / fatigue / exercise tolerance); physical-activity counseling; alcohol; tobacco — — CHA2DS2-VASc reassessment; HAS-BLED reassessment for modifiable bleeding risk; OSA screen (annual at minimum); CPAP adherence — LAAO (Watchman) candidacy reassessment when OAC contraindicated or major bleed; structured weight-loss program for BMI ≥27

Summary recommendation

Primary-care monitoring of established atrial fibrillation / flutter is anchored in annual (and event-driven) reassessment of CHA2DS2-VASc thromboembolic risk and modifiable bleeding-risk factors via HAS-BLED (used to identify, not to withhold, anticoagulation).1 DOACs are preferred over warfarin in non-valvular AF; warfarin patients require ongoing INR monitoring (target 2.0–3.0; goal time-in-therapeutic-range >70%), with conversion to a DOAC considered when TTR cannot be maintained.1 DOAC patients need baseline and at least annual creatinine / eGFR (more frequent — approximately every CrCl/10 months — at lower CrCl, age ≥75, frailty, or acute illness), plus periodic hepatic and hemoglobin assessment; FDA-labeled dosing must be used (non-evidence-based dose reductions are explicitly discouraged, Class 3 Harm).1 Every visit should include pulse / rhythm assessment with ECG when needed; rate control accepts resting HR <110 bpm (lenient, per RACE II) when symptoms are controlled and LV function preserved, with stricter HR <80 bpm if symptomatic or tachycardia-mediated cardiomyopathy suspected. TTE is obtained at diagnosis and repeated for clinical change or pre-procedure. Post-ablation patients are followed with a 3-month blanking period before judging success, with symptom-driven ambulatory rhythm monitoring; OAC continues based on CHA2DS2-VASc regardless of apparent ablation success.1 The 2023 guideline elevates lifestyle and risk-factor modification to a co-equal pillar: ≥10% weight loss for BMI ≥27, ~210 min/wk moderate-vigorous exercise, BP <130/80, optimized glycemic and lipid management, alcohol minimization, tobacco cessation, and OSA screening / treatment.

Laboratory

  • INR (warfarin) — at least monthly once stable; more often during initiation, dose changes, or interacting drug / illness. Target 2.0–3.0; goal TTR >70%; consider switch to DOAC if TTR cannot be maintained.1
  • Renal function (serum creatinine, eGFR / Cockcroft-Gault CrCl) for DOAC dosing — at baseline and at least annually; more frequent at CrCl <60 mL/min, age ≥75, frailty, intercurrent illness (every CrCl/10 months).1
  • Hepatic function (AST / ALT, bilirubin) — baseline; periodically thereafter (per package-insert guidance). Avoid DOAC in moderate–severe hepatic impairment.1
  • CBC (hemoglobin) — at least annually; sooner with symptoms. Investigate unexplained Hgb drop on OAC.1
  • Diabetes (HbA1c) and lipid panel — per ADA / ACC-AHA prevention cadence.1

Imaging

  • 12-lead ECG / rhythm strip — at every clinical visit when rhythm assessment indicated; for symptoms or change.1
  • Transthoracic echocardiogram — at initial AF diagnosis; repeat for change in clinical status, new murmur, suspected progression of LV dysfunction, or before / after cardioversion / ablation / LAAO.1
  • TEE or pre-procedure cardiac CT — prior to cardioversion when <3 weeks of therapeutic OAC; pre-ablation; pre-LAAO per protocol.1
  • Ambulatory rhythm monitoring (Holter, patch, event monitor, ILR) — routine clinical follow-up at ≥3 months post-ablation; arrhythmia within the 3-month blanking period not counted as failure; ongoing intermittent monitoring as symptoms dictate.1

Physical examination

  • Resting heart rate / pulse and rhythm — every visit. Rate-control target resting HR <110 bpm (lenient) reasonable when asymptomatic and LVEF preserved; stricter HR <80 bpm if symptoms persist or tachycardia-mediated cardiomyopathy.1
  • Blood pressure — every visit. Target <130/80 mm Hg.1
  • Weight / BMI — every visit; structured weight-loss program for BMI ≥27 (target ≥10% body-weight reduction).1

Functional / surveys

  • CHA2DS2-VASc — annually and with any change in clinical status. Anticoagulate when score ≥2 (men) or ≥3 (women); shared decision for score 1 (men) / 2 (women). Annual documented stroke-risk assessment is a 2026 AHA/ACC AF performance measure.14
  • HAS-BLED (or equivalent bleeding-risk score) — annually and when risk profile changes. Used to flag modifiable factors (uncontrolled BP, NSAIDs, alcohol, labile INR), NOT to withhold OAC.1
  • Symptom assessment (palpitations, dyspnea, fatigue, exercise tolerance; EHRA / CCS-SAF) — every visit; drives rate-vs.-rhythm-control decisions and escalation.1
  • Physical-activity counseling — every visit. Target ~210 min/week moderate-vigorous activity. Secondary-prevention risk-factor management (weight, activity, smoking, alcohol, BP) is a 2026 AHA/ACC AF performance measure.14
  • Alcohol-use assessment and reduction counseling — every visit. Minimize or eliminate (≤1 drink/day, abstinence preferred for rhythm-control goals).1
  • Tobacco-use assessment and cessation counseling — every visit.1
  • OSA symptom screen, with sleep study and CPAP adherence review if diagnosed — at diagnosis and at least annually; treatment adherence reviewed each visit.1
  • LAA occlusion (Watchman) candidacy — at any OAC contraindication / major bleed; Medicare coverage (NCD 20.34, CED) requires CHADS2 ≥2 or CHA2DS2-VASc ≥3, documented shared decision-making with a non-interventional physician, and inability to take long-term OAC.13

Notes

  • Screening for AF in asymptomatic adults. USPSTF 2022 issued an I (insufficient evidence) statement for screening ≥50 without prior AF; 2023 ACC/AHA/ACCP/HRS endorses opportunistic pulse / rhythm screening in adults ≥65 (Class 2a) and is more permissive about consumer wearables.12
  • Heart-rate target lenient vs. strict. RACE II established non-inferiority of lenient (<110 bpm) vs. strict; 2023 guideline endorses lenient as reasonable (Class 2a). Stricter remains appropriate for reduced LV function, persistent symptoms, or tachycardia-mediated cardiomyopathy.1
  • LAAO (Watchman) eligibility. 2023 guideline upgraded LAAO to Class 2a (broader than 2019 Class 2b). CMS NCD 20.34 adds CED requirements (operator / site volumes, registry enrollment).13
  • DOACs in advanced CKD / ESRD. 2023 guideline allows labeled-dose apixaban (and warfarin) in stage 4–5 CKD / dialysis (Class 2a–2b); earlier nephrology positions preferred warfarin. Randomized data remain limited.1

Footnotes

  1. Joglar JA et al. 2023 ACC/AHA/ACCP/HRS Guideline for the Diagnosis and Management of Atrial Fibrillation. Circulation. 2024;149(1):e1-e156. See bibliography.md → accaha-af-2023. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

  3. CMS. National Coverage Determination 20.34: Percutaneous Left Atrial Appendage Closure. See bibliography.md → cms-laac-ncd. ↩↩

  4. Benjamin E, Marcus G, Hess P, et al. 2026 AHA/ACC Clinical Performance and Quality Measures for Patients With Atrial Fibrillation. J Am Coll Cardiol. Published online August 17, 2026. doi:10.1016/j.jacc.2026.05.032. https://www.jacc.org/doi/10.1016/j.jacc.2026.05.032 See bibliography.md → aha-acc-af-pm-2026.

Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease

ICD-10: J44.x (COPD; with / without exacerbation; with emphysema; with chronic bronchitis) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 12.8% — rank 14 of 30 Category: Respiratory Primary associations consulted: GOLD, USPSTF, ATS, CMS, CDC, ALA

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — — — Spirometry (post-bronchodilator FEV1 / FVC and FEV1 % predicted — at diagnosis + at least annually to detect rapid decliners); vaccination review (influenza annually; COVID-19 per current CDC season; PCV20 or PCV21 single dose ≥50; RSV single dose ≥75 or 50–74 with COPD; Tdap; zoster ≥50) — Alpha-1 antitrypsin (AAT) once in lifetime at diagnosis; blood eosinophil count at baseline + when considering ICS escalation / de-escalation; ABG when SpO2 ≤92% or for LTOT qualification
Imaging — — — — — Non-contrast chest CT at least once after diagnosis (GOLD 2027); repeat for frequent exacerbations / persistent symptoms / suspected lung cancer, emphysema, bronchiectasis; annual LDCT lung-cancer screening if ≥20 pack-years, age 50–80 (USPSTF Grade B); LVR / transplant evaluation
Physical exam Pulse oximetry (SpO2); smoking status + cessation counseling; BMI / nutritional status — — — — ECG at diagnosis and with unexplained symptom change; NT-proBNP if HF suspected; CV risk reassessment after exacerbations (GOLD 2027)
Functional / surveys CAT (COPD Assessment Test); mMRC dyspnea; exacerbation history (12-mo lookback); GOLD A / B / E reclassification — — Pulmonary-rehab eligibility review (after exacerbation hospitalization initiate within 3 wk of discharge; stable mMRC ≥2 / CAT ≥10) — 6-Minute Walk Test at pulmonary-rehab entry / exit; LTOT assessment (resting PaO2 ≤55 / SpO2 ≤88 — or ≤60 / 88 with cor pulmonale / polycythemia — confirm twice ≥3 wk apart)

Summary recommendation

US primary-care COPD surveillance under GOLD 2027 emphasizes structured, symptom- and exacerbation-driven monitoring rather than dense laboratory testing.1 At every visit, clinicians should assess symptoms with the CAT (≥10 = "more symptoms") and mMRC dyspnea scale (≥2 triggering pulmonary-rehab referral), document moderate and severe exacerbations over the past 12 months to reclassify GOLD A / B / E groupings (a single moderate exacerbation now places a patient in group E), measure SpO2, and reinforce smoking cessation.1 Spirometry is performed at diagnosis and at least annually thereafter (or with significant clinical change) to identify rapid decliners. Alpha-1 antitrypsin testing should be obtained once in every COPD patient, and a baseline blood eosinophil count informs ICS and biologic (dupilumab / mepolizumab) decisions, with ≥300 cells/µL favoring ICS / biologic therapy and <100 cells/µL arguing against ICS.1 Vaccinations (annual influenza; COVID-19 per current CDC season; PCV20 or PCV21 single dose for all ≥50 and younger adults with chronic lung disease; RSV single dose for all ≥75 and 50–74 with COPD; Tdap; zoster) follow CDC and should be reviewed yearly789. Pulmonary rehabilitation should be initiated within 3 weeks of any exacerbation-related hospitalization (ATS 2023 strong recommendation) and offered to stable patients with mMRC ≥2; CMS NCD 240.8 covers up to 36 sessions for GOLD II–IV COPD.26 Long-term oxygen therapy is initiated when stable resting PaO2 ≤55 mm Hg or SpO2 ≤88% (or ≤60 mm Hg / 88% with cor pulmonale or polycythemia), confirmed on two measurements ≥3 weeks apart, with a treatment target SpO2 88–92%.13

Laboratory

  • Spirometry (post-bronchodilator FEV1 / FVC, FEV1 % predicted) — at diagnosis; at least annually thereafter to detect rapid decliners and with significant clinical change. Post-BD FEV1 / FVC <0.70 confirms airflow limitation.1
  • Pulse oximetry (SpO2) — every visit; ABG if SpO2 ≤92% or for LTOT qualification.1
  • Arterial blood gas (ABG) — as indicated (SpO2 ≤92%, LTOT assessment, suspected hypercapnia / NIV consideration). PaO2 ≤55 mm Hg (or ≤60 with cor pulmonale / polycythemia) qualifies for LTOT; confirm twice ≥3 weeks apart.13
  • Alpha-1 antitrypsin (AAT) — once in lifetime at COPD diagnosis (also adult-onset asthma with persistent obstruction, unexplained bronchiectasis). Serum AAT <11 µmol/L (<57 mg/dL) = severe deficiency.1
  • Blood eosinophil count — baseline and when considering ICS escalation / de-escalation. ≥300 favors ICS (consider LABA+LAMA+ICS as initial therapy in group E) and, with exacerbations despite triple therapy, supports dupilumab (if chronic bronchitis) or mepolizumab (with or without chronic bronchitis) add-on; 100–300 supports ICS if exacerbating; <100 argues against ICS.1

Imaging

  • Chest CT (non-contrast, inspiratory + expiratory) — at least once in all patients with COPD where available (emphysema, bronchiectasis, mucus plugging, ILA, coronary calcium, bone density; does not replace spirometry); repeat for frequent exacerbations, persistent symptoms despite optimized therapy, LVR evaluation. Annual LDCT lung-cancer screening if ≥20 pack-years, age 50–80 (USPSTF 2021 Grade B).15

Physical examination

  • Pulse oximetry, smoking status, BMI / nutritional status — every visit. Identify low BMI (<21 kg/m²) and unintentional weight loss as poor-prognosis markers.1
  • ECG and cardiovascular assessment — ECG at COPD diagnosis and with unexplained symptom change (Holter if intermittent dizziness / syncope); NT-proBNP / BNP if heart failure suspected, then echo if abnormal; CV risk assessment at baseline and after exacerbations (standard calculators underestimate risk in COPD; CT-derived coronary calcium adds information).1

Functional / surveys

  • CAT (COPD Assessment Test) — every clinic visit. CAT ≥10 = "more symptoms" (group B or E); change ≥2 is clinically meaningful.1
  • mMRC dyspnea scale — every clinic visit. mMRC ≥2 = "more symptoms" and a trigger for pulmonary-rehab referral.1
  • Exacerbation history (moderate / severe events in past 12 months) — every visit. Defines group E (≥1 moderate or severe exacerbation in the past 12 months). A single moderate exacerbation prompts treatment escalation toward a low-disease-activity state with no exacerbations.1
  • Smoking-cessation counseling — every visit (behavioral + pharmacotherapy first-line).1
  • Vaccination status review — annually; aligned to CDC (influenza annually; COVID-19 per current CDC season; PCV20 or PCV21 single dose — all ≥50, 19–49 with chronic lung disease; RSV single dose, not repeated — all ≥75, 50–74 with COPD or other risk condition; Tdap; zoster ≥50).1789
  • 6-Minute Walk Test (6MWT) — at pulmonary-rehab entry / exit; LTOT assessment; LVR / transplant evaluation.12
  • Pulmonary rehabilitation referral — after any exacerbation hospitalization (initiate within 3 weeks of discharge); stable patients with mMRC ≥2 / CAT ≥10; GOLD 2027 also advises PR assessment at initial evaluation for groups B and E and for dyspnea at follow-up. CMS NCD 240.8 covers 36 sessions for GOLD II–IV.216

Notes

  • Screening vs. case-finding. USPSTF 2022 (Grade D) recommends against spirometric screening of asymptomatic adults. GOLD 2026 / 2027 endorses targeted case-finding (questionnaire / risk tool, then spirometry) in adults reporting cough, sputum, dyspnea, recurrent LRI, or significant tobacco / biomass exposure, including people enrolled in lung-cancer screening10. Both agree against screening truly asymptomatic adults.41
  • Eosinophil thresholds for ICS. GOLD uses a graded approach (≥300 favors, <100 disfavors). Some specialty bodies emphasize a single ≥300 cells/µL cut-point and weight exacerbation history over eosinophils.1
  • Pulmonary-rehab eligibility timing. ATS 2023 strongly recommends PR within 3 weeks of hospital discharge. CMS NCD 240.8 restricts coverage to GOLD II–IV (moderate–very severe); does not cover GOLD I, even though GOLD / ATS would consider PR for symptomatic mild disease.26
  • Annual spirometry. GOLD 2027 states spirometry "should be repeated at least annually" to detect rapid decline; some primary-care guidance considers it optional in stable, mildly symptomatic patients.1

Heart Failure

ICD-10: I50.x (heart failure — systolic, diastolic, mixed; with / without RHF) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 12.3% — rank 15 of 30 Category: Cardiovascular Primary associations consulted: AHA/ACC/HFSA, HFSA, CMS, NHLBI

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — BMP (Na, K, BUN, Cr / eGFR, Mg) within 1–2 wk after starting / titrating ACEi / ARB / ARNI / MRA / SGLT2i / diuretic; finerenone K + eGFR at 4 wk post start / titration; every 3–6 mo when stable BMP every 3–6 mo (stable) Iron studies (ferritin, TSAT) — HFrEF periodic; lipid panel; HbA1c (if DM); TSH; CBC; urinalysis if clinically indicated — Initial labs at diagnosis (CBC, urinalysis, BMP, LFTs, lipid, TSH, HbA1c, iron studies); BNP / NT-proBNP at presentation, admission, and predischarge; troponin with acute change
Imaging — — — — — TTE at diagnosis; repeat with unexplained clinical change, after intervention likely to alter LV function (3–6 mo after GDMT optimization for HFimpEF), or pre-procedure; ECG at initial eval + with symptom change or device consideration
Physical exam BP, HR, weight, JVP, peripheral edema, lung exam (volume status); NYHA functional class — — — — —
Functional / surveys NYHA class; weight diary / RPM review KCCQ (Kansas City Cardiomyopathy Questionnaire) every 3–6 mo or with clinical change — Depression screen (PHQ-2 → PHQ-9); cardiac-rehab participation review — Cardiac-rehab referral (42 CFR §410.49: LVEF ≤35%, NYHA II–IV, OMT ≥6 wk, no hospitalization / procedure within 6 wk); RPM; CardioMEMS / IPAPS (Medicare CED-only, NCD 20.36)

Summary recommendation

US primary-care surveillance of established adult heart failure (HFrEF, HFmrEF, HFpEF) under the 2022 AHA/ACC/HFSA guideline (with the 2024 HFrEF and 2026 HFpEF ACC ECDPs and 2024 Performance Measures) follows a structured cadence.12 Every visit captures BP, HR, weight, volume status (JVP / edema / lungs), and NYHA class; KCCQ is recommended at baseline and serially to track patient-reported health status (Class 2a).1 During GDMT initiation or titration of ACEi / ARB / ARNI / MRA or finerenone / SGLT2i or diuretic7, a BMP (Na, K, Cr / eGFR, BUN, Mg) is checked within 1–2 weeks, then every 3–6 months once stable; stable patients are otherwise seen every 3–6 months.12 Initial workup includes CBC, BMP, LFTs, lipids, TSH, HbA1c, iron studies (ferritin / TSAT), urinalysis, ECG, and 2D echocardiogram with Doppler (Class 1).1 BNP or NT-proBNP is Class 1 for diagnosis and for hospital admission / predischarge prognostication; outpatient serial measurement to titrate therapy is not endorsed but values inform prognosis and decongestion. Iron studies are repeated periodically in HFrEF; IV iron is indicated when ferritin <100 ng/mL or 100–300 with TSAT <20%. Echocardiography is repeated only with unexplained clinical change or after an intervention expected to alter LV function (e.g., to document HFimpEF after 3–6 months of GDMT) — routine surveillance imaging is not recommended. CMS covers cardiac rehabilitation for stable HFrEF (LVEF ≤35%, NYHA II–IV) under 42 CFR §410.49 (NCD 20.10 retired), and supports RPM (weight, BP, PA pressure) for chronic HF management.4

Laboratory

  • Initial labs (at diagnosis) — CBC, urinalysis, BMP (Na, K, BUN, Cr, eGFR, glucose), LFTs, lipid panel, TSH, iron studies (ferritin, TSAT), HbA1c. Identify comorbidities and reversible causes.1
  • BNP or NT-proBNP — diagnostic — at presentation with dyspnea or suspected HF.1
  • BNP or NT-proBNP — prognostic — at hospital admission and predischarge; selectively in outpatient follow-up.1
  • BMP (Na, K, Cr, eGFR, BUN, Mg) post-GDMT — within 1–2 weeks after starting or titrating ACEi / ARB / ARNI / MRA (steroidal or finerenone) / SGLT2i / diuretic; then every 3–6 months when stable. K 4.0–5.0 mEq/L; tolerate up to ~30% rise in Cr on RAAS initiation; address hyperkalemia >5.5. Finerenone (LVEF ≥40%): label requires K + eGFR before start, at 4 weeks after start and each dose change, then periodically; do not start if K >5.0 or eGFR <25; withhold / reduce for K ≥5.5 per label.127
  • Iron studies (ferritin, TSAT) — at diagnosis and periodically (e.g., annually) in HFrEF; reassess after IV iron repletion. IV iron indicated when ferritin <100 ng/mL or 100–300 ng/mL with TSAT <20%.1
  • Troponin — with acute clinical change or suspected ACS.1

Imaging

  • 2D transthoracic echocardiogram with Doppler — at initial diagnosis; repeat with significant unexplained change in clinical status or after intervention likely to alter LV function (e.g., 3–6 months after GDMT optimization to document HFimpEF). NOT routine surveillance. Classify LVEF (HFrEF ≤40%, HFmrEF 41–49%, HFpEF ≥50%; HFimpEF if ≥10-point rise to >40%).1
  • 12-lead ECG — at initial evaluation; repeat with symptom change, arrhythmia, or QRS-related device consideration. Identify rhythm, conduction (QRS ≥150 ms LBBB for CRT), ischemia.1
  • Implantable pulmonary artery pressure sensor (CardioMEMS / IPAPS) — daily transmitted pressures with HF-team review. AHA/ACC/HFSA 2022: Class 2b (NYHA III + HF hospitalization in prior 12 mo). Medicare (NCD 20.36, effective 2025-01-13) covers only within a CMS-approved CED study: NYHA II–III, chronic HF ≥3 mo, HF hospitalization / urgent visit within 12 mo OR elevated natriuretic peptide within 30 days, GDMT ≥3 mo, ICD / CRT evaluation ≥3 mo prior, no major CV event within 3 mo. 2026 ACC HFpEF ECDP: consider in selected high-risk HFpEF with persistent symptoms or recurrent hospitalization.163

Physical examination

  • BP, HR, weight, JVP, peripheral edema, lung exam — every visit (every 1–2 weeks during GDMT titration; every 3–6 months when stable). Target euvolemia.1

Functional / surveys

  • NYHA functional class — every visit.1
  • KCCQ (Kansas City Cardiomyopathy Questionnaire) — baseline and serially (every 3–6 months or with clinical change). Score 0–100; <50 indicates poor-to-fair health status.1
  • Cardiac rehabilitation participation — periodic exercise-capacity reassessment. Medicare (42 CFR §410.49; NCD 20.10 retired) covers LVEF ≤35%, NYHA II–IV on optimal therapy ≥6 weeks, no hospitalization / procedure within 6 weeks.14
  • Depression screen (PHQ-2 → PHQ-9) — periodically.5

Notes

  • Routine echocardiography. 2022 guideline explicitly rejects routine serial echo in stable patients; community practice often obtains annual echoes. Echo at 3–6 months after GDMT optimization is supported only to document HFimpEF or guide device decisions.1
  • Serial NT-proBNP for therapy titration. 2022 guideline states evidence is insufficient for biomarker-guided titration (GUIDE-IT trial neutral); some HFSA expert pathways still endorse trend monitoring.1
  • SGLT2i in HFpEF. 2022 guideline gave Class 2a (EMPEROR-Preserved). The 2026 ACC HFpEF ECDP (replaces 2023) designates SGLT2i plus a nonsteroidal MRA (finerenone; FDA-approved for LVEF ≥40% July 2025) as foundational unless contraindicated, with incretin therapy for BMI ≥30 and SBP target ~120–129 mm Hg. Formal CoR in the 2022 guideline remains 2a; no focused update was issued, and the next full AHA/ACC HF guideline is expected late 2027.1378
  • Iron deficiency thresholds. AHA/ACC/HFSA 2022 and ESC 2021 both use ferritin <100 OR (100–300 with TSAT <20%), but newer analyses argue TSAT <20% with serum iron <13 µmol/L better predicts response — threshold under debate.1
  • CardioMEMS / implantable PA monitoring. Class 2b "value uncertain" reflects discordant CHAMPION (positive) and GUIDE-HF (neutral) results; specialty HF groups push for broader use. CMS NCD 20.36 (2025) now provides CED coverage for NYHA II–III — wider than the guideline's NYHA III Class 2b (federal vs. society scope difference).16
  • LVEF categories. The Second Universal Definition of HF (AHA/ACC/ESC/WHF with HFSA, June 2026) moves away from strict LVEF cutoffs, accounts for LVEF differences by sex, age, and ethnicity, and uses reduced / preserved / improved EF. The 2022 guideline cutoffs (≤40 / 41–49 / ≥50) are retained here pending the 2027 guideline.81

Osteoporosis

ICD-10: M80.x (osteoporosis with current pathologic fracture), M81.x (osteoporosis without fracture) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 11.5% — rank 16 of 30 Category: Musculoskeletal Primary associations consulted: USPSTF, AACE/ACE, BHOF (formerly NOF), Endocrine Society, ACP, CMS

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — Serum Ca prior to each IV zoledronate or denosumab dose — 25-OH vitamin D (after repletion, then annually); calcium-intake assessment — Baseline secondary-causes workup (Ca, 25-OH vit D, PTH, alk phos, Cr / eGFR, P, Mg, TSH, CBC, 24-hr urine Ca; SPEP / celiac / testosterone if indicated); CTX or P1NP bone-turnover markers (baseline + 3–6 mo after starting / changing therapy, and during drug holiday); eGFR before denosumab — if eGFR <30 / dialysis, CKD-MBD evaluation and close post-dose Ca monitoring (weeks 2–10; FDA Boxed Warning)
Imaging — — — — Central DXA every 2 years (Medicare-coverable when medically necessary; USPSTF acknowledges 4–8 yr intervals may suffice in low-risk untreated women) Central DXA every 1–2 yr on therapy (AACE) or 1–3 yr (Endo Society) until stable, then longer intervals; Vertebral Fracture Assessment (VFA) if height loss ≥1.5 in historical / ≥0.8 in prospective, kyphosis, recent low-trauma fracture, chronic glucocorticoids, or T-score ≤ −2.5
Physical exam — — — Standing height measurement; fall-risk screen (Timed Up and Go, fall history, medication review, vision, gait / balance) — Height drop ≥2 cm prospective or ≥4 cm historical triggers VFA
Functional / surveys — — Denosumab continuity check (no gap >7 mo; if discontinuing, transition to bisphosphonate to prevent rebound vertebral fractures) FRAX 10-yr fracture risk recalculation; calcium intake (target 1000 mg/d men 50–70, 1200 mg/d women ≥51 / men ≥71; vit D 800–1000 IU/d) — Drug-holiday eligibility reassessment after 5 yr oral bisphosphonate or 3 yr IV zoledronate; anabolic-to-antiresorptive sequencing at end of teriparatide / abaloparatide (18–24 mo) or romosozumab (12 mo)

Summary recommendation

Primary-care osteoporosis surveillance combines BMD imaging, fracture-risk recalibration, drug-class-specific safety checks, and lifestyle / fall assessment. Per USPSTF 2025 (Grade B), screening DXA is recommended for women ≥65 and for postmenopausal women <65 at increased risk; men remain an I-statement.1 Once on pharmacotherapy, AACE 2020 endorses repeating central DXA every 1–2 years until stable, with longer intervals thereafter; the Endocrine Society 2019 supports 1–3 years; ACP 2023 and BHOF 2022 take a more skeptical stance on within-treatment DXA because fracture-risk reduction is decoupled from measured BMD change.2356 FRAX should be recalculated when risk factors change and at drug-holiday decision points. Drug-class monitoring is critical: denosumab must not be discontinued without immediate bisphosphonate transition (rebound vertebral fractures); oral bisphosphonates warrant drug-holiday consideration after 5 years (IV zoledronate after 3 years) in low-to-moderate-risk patients; anabolic agents (teriparatide, abaloparatide, romosozumab) require sequencing to an antiresorptive at course end.32 All patients need baseline secondary-causes labs (Ca, 25-OH vit D, PTH, alkaline phosphatase, creatinine, TSH, ±24-hr urine Ca), pre-dose calcium checks for IV antiresorptives, and ongoing calcium 1000–1200 mg/d plus vitamin D 800–1000 IU/d. Annual height measurement, fall-risk screening, and vertebral fracture assessment when triggered round out primary-care surveillance. CTX / P1NP markers offer an inexpensive adherence and response check 3–6 months after therapy change and during drug holidays.

Laboratory

  • Secondary-causes workup at diagnosis — serum Ca, 25-OH vitamin D, PTH, alkaline phosphatase, Cr / eGFR, phosphorus, magnesium, TSH, CBC, 24-hour urine Ca; consider SPEP, celiac serology, testosterone (men). Identify reversible causes; correct hypocalcemia and vitamin D deficiency before antiresorptive therapy.263
  • 25-OH vitamin D — at baseline; repeat after repletion until adequate, then annually. Target ≥30 ng/mL.26
  • Serum calcium (corrected) — prior to each IV zoledronate or denosumab dose; before initiating any antiresorptive. Avoid hypocalcemia, especially with CKD. Denosumab (FDA Boxed Warning 2024): assess eGFR before prescribing; if eGFR <30 / dialysis, evaluate for CKD-MBD (nephrology input), correct Ca and replete Ca + activated vit D before dosing, and monitor serum Ca closely after each dose (peak risk weeks 2–5, range 2–10).239
  • Bone-turnover markers — serum CTX (antiresorptive) or P1NP (anabolic) — baseline; 3–6 months after starting / changing therapy; during drug holiday. Suppression of CTX with antiresorptive; rise in P1NP with anabolic indicates response; rise during holiday signals waning effect.236

Imaging

  • Central DXA (lumbar spine + hip / femoral neck) for screening — every 2 years (Medicare-coverable when medically necessary; USPSTF 2025 acknowledges 4–8 year intervals may suffice in untreated low-risk women).17
  • Central DXA for treatment monitoring — every 1–2 years (AACE) or 1–3 years (Endocrine Society) until stable, then less frequently. ACP 2023 and BHOF 2022 do not endorse routine repeat DXA during the initial 5-year bisphosphonate course because fracture benefit is decoupled from measured BMD change.2356
  • Vertebral Fracture Assessment (VFA) via DXA or lateral spine radiograph — at baseline if height loss ≥1.5 in (4 cm) historical or ≥0.8 in (2 cm) prospective, kyphosis, recent low-trauma fracture, chronic glucocorticoids, or T-score ≤ −2.5; repeat as clinically warranted.62

Physical examination

  • Standing height measurement (stadiometer) — annually for all postmenopausal women and men ≥50. Trigger VFA if loss ≥2 cm prospective or ≥4 cm historical.62
  • Fall-risk assessment (Timed Up and Go, history of falls, medication review, vision, gait / balance) — annually. TUG <12 sec; identify and mitigate modifiable risks.62

Functional / surveys

  • FRAX 10-year fracture risk recalculation — at baseline and as indicated when risk factors change; at drug-holiday decision points. Major osteoporotic ≥20% or hip ≥3% triggers treatment. USPSTF 2025: in postmenopausal women <65 with risk factors, use a validated tool (FRAX, OST, ORAI, SCORE) to select for DXA; FRAX has no USPSTF screening threshold; race-specific FRAX calculators may underestimate risk in Asian, Black, and Hispanic patients.126
  • Calcium-intake assessment (dietary ± supplement) — at baseline and annually. Target 1000 mg/d men 50–70, 1200 mg/d women ≥51 / men ≥71; avoid >1200–1500 mg/d.623
  • Drug-holiday eligibility reassessment — after 5 years oral bisphosphonate or 3 years IV zoledronate in low-to-moderate ongoing fracture risk. Holiday up to 5 yr (oral) or 3 yr (zoledronate) with reassessment every 2–4 yr. Do NOT holiday high-risk or denosumab patients.3265
  • Denosumab continuity check — every 6 months at dosing visits. No gap >7 months; if discontinuing, transition to bisphosphonate to prevent rebound vertebral fractures.326
  • Anabolic-to-antiresorptive sequencing — at end of 18–24 mo anabolic course (teriparatide / abaloparatide) or 12 mo romosozumab. Follow with bisphosphonate or denosumab to preserve BMD gains.426

Notes

  • Screening starting age in younger postmenopausal women. USPSTF 2025 limits Grade B screening to postmenopausal women <65 with elevated risk identified by clinical tool (now favoring DXA over tool-only triage); AACE and BHOF screen all postmenopausal women ≥50 with risk factors and all women ≥65 regardless of risk.126
  • Men. USPSTF 2025 issues an I-statement for men; AACE and BHOF support screening men ≥70 (and ≥50 with risk factors). The Endocrine Society 2012 men's guideline was retired Oct 2025 without replacement and is no longer an operative source.128
  • DXA on therapy. Endocrine Society 1–3 years; AACE 1–2 years until stable; ACP 2023 (reaffirmed 2025) and BHOF 2022 recommend against routine BMD monitoring10 during initial 5-year bisphosphonate course because fracture benefit accrues independent of BMD change.2356
  • Bone-turnover markers. AACE and Endocrine Society endorse CTX / P1NP for response / adherence monitoring; ACP (2023; reaffirmed 2025) does not recommend routine use10; BHOF describes them as an optional adjunct.235
  • Screening interval untreated. USPSTF acknowledges evidence-poor area, with modeling suggesting 4–8 year repeat intervals in low-risk women; CMS reimburses every 2 years (every 23 months) and more often if medically necessary.17
  • AACE update pending. AACE lists its 2020 postmenopausal osteoporosis guideline as under revision (update in development as of Nov 2026); re-audit DXA-on-therapy intervals on publication.2

Prostate Cancer

ICD-10: C61 (malignant neoplasm of prostate) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 9.9% (males only) — rank 17 of 30 Category: Cancer (urologic; males only) Primary associations consulted: NCCN, AUA/SUO, ASCO, ASTRO, USPSTF

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — PSA on active surveillance (q3–6 mo NCCN; AUA ≥q6 mo); PSA post-curative q3–6 mo first 2 yr (AUA); testosterone on ADT (q3–6 mo, target <50 ng/dL); PSA on ADT / AR-pathway inhibitor / nmCRPC; abiraterone LFTs / K+ / BP (q2 wk × 3 mo then monthly); mCRPC baseline labs (CBC, CMP, ALP, LDH, Hgb) each cycle PSA post-curative (q6 mo years 2–5) PSA post-curative (after 5 yr stable, annual); lipid panel + fasting glucose / HbA1c (on ADT); Ca / 25-OH vit D (on ADT, on bone-modifying agents) DEXA on ADT (baseline, at 1 yr, then every 1–2 yr); MRI prostate on AS (every 1–3 yr) Confirmatory biopsy on AS within ~12 mo (MRI first if not MRI-guided), then every 1–4 yr or as triggered; germline testing (all advanced) + somatic (metastatic); PSMA PET/CT preferred at BCR, or PSMA PET vs CT + bone scan for unfavorable intermediate / high-risk staging; conventional CT abd/pelvis + Tc-99m bone scan at BCR / progression; imaging in nmCRPC q6–12 mo, mCRPC at least annually; eGFR / Cr per agent
Imaging — — — — DEXA on ADT (baseline, at 1 yr, then every 1–2 yr); mpMRI on AS (every 1–3 yr) PSMA PET/CT preferred at BCR after local therapy; PSMA PET or CT + bone scan for unfavorable intermediate / high-risk staging; CT abd/pelvis + bone scan at progression; nmCRPC imaging q6–12 mo; mCRPC imaging at least annually (sooner if PSADT short or symptoms)
Physical exam BP and cardiovascular assessment on ADT / AR-pathway inhibitor — — DRE on active surveillance; DRE post-curative (may omit if PSA undetectable post-RP) — —
Functional / surveys Distress screen (NCCN Distress Thermometer); hot flashes / vasomotor symptoms (on ADT); mood / depression; sexual function review — — Urinary continence (EPIC, IPSS); erectile function (SHIM / IIEF); bowel function post-RT; age-appropriate second-cancer screening (CRC, skin) per USPSTF — Urology referral for bothersome incontinence or persistent rectal bleeding

Summary recommendation

The dominant rhythm of prostate cancer surveillance is PSA every 3–6 months with risk-adapted intensification. On active surveillance, NCCN v5.2026 uses PSA every 3–6 months while AUA/ASTRO (2022; amended 2026) advises PSA generally no more often than every 6 months (conflict flagged; NCCN cadence retained); annual DRE (AUA allows every 1–2 years); MRI if the diagnostic biopsy was not MRI-guided and roughly every 1–3 years thereafter; a confirmatory biopsy within ~12 months (promptly for PI-RADS 4–5), then surveillance biopsies every 1–4 years or when triggered by PSA rise, new DRE findings, or rising PI-RADS — MRI does not replace periodic biopsy12.12 After curative therapy, PSA is checked every 3–6 months for the first 2 years, every 6 months in years 2–5, and then annually (AUA/ASTRO 2026; more frequent than the NCCN / ACS 6–12-month schedule and adopted as the conservative interval)2; the operative definitions of recurrence are PSA ≥0.2 ng/mL (confirmed) post-prostatectomy and PSA nadir + 2 ng/mL (Phoenix) post-radiation. PSMA PET is preferred at PSA recurrence after exhaustion of local therapy (AUA/SUO 2026) and is an equal alternative to bone scan + cross-sectional imaging for staging unfavorable intermediate / high-risk disease (AUA/ASTRO 2026)32.31 For men on ADT or AR-pathway inhibitors (apalutamide, enzalutamide, darolutamide, abiraterone), primary-care-relevant monitoring includes serum testosterone (goal <50 ng/dL), DEXA + FRAX at baseline, repeated 1 year after starting ADT and every 1–2 years thereafter, with FRAX-guided bisphosphonate or denosumab use3, calcium / vitamin D replacement, annual lipid and glycemic screening, blood pressure at every visit, and structured assessment of hot flashes, fatigue, mood, sexual function, and cognition.36 In mCRPC, imaging is performed at least annually even with stable PSA. Survivorship priorities the PCP owns include urinary and sexual function, depression and PSA anxiety, cardiometabolic risk on ADT, bone health, and continued age-appropriate screening for second cancers.67

Laboratory

  • PSA on active surveillance — every 3–6 months (NCCN). AUA/ASTRO 2026 advises PSA generally no more often than every 6 months; interval may be individualized (less intense with low progression risk / limited life expectancy). Rising PSA: repeat PSA first, then MRI ± biopsy. PSA density >0.15 ng/mL/cc triggers re-evaluation.12
  • PSA post-radical prostatectomy — every 3–6 months for the first 2 years, every 6 months years 2–5, then annually (AUA/ASTRO 2026; NCCN / ACS allow 6–12 months × 5 yr). Target undetectable (<0.1 ng/mL; ultrasensitive <0.01); BCR = 2 consecutive PSA ≥0.2 ng/mL. Beyond 10 yr with undetectable PSA, duration is a shared decision.216
  • PSA post-radiation — every 3–6 months for the first 2 years, every 6 months years 2–5, then annually (AUA/ASTRO 2026; NCCN / ACS allow 6–12 months × 5 yr). BCR = PSA nadir + 2 ng/mL (Phoenix).216
  • Testosterone on ADT — every 3–6 months. Target <50 ng/dL (castrate); castrate level required for CRPC definition.31
  • PSA on ADT / AR-pathway inhibitor — every 3–6 months. Declining / stable expected; rising PSA = progression to CRPC if T<50.31
  • mCRPC baseline labs (CBC, CMP, ALP, LDH, Hgb) — at baseline and with each treatment cycle / visit. ALP rise suggests bone progression.3
  • Abiraterone monitoring (LFTs, potassium, BP) — every 2 weeks × first 3 months, then monthly. Also applies to high-risk localized / cN1 disease treated with RT + ADT + abiraterone / prednisone for 24 months (AUA/ASTRO 2026).32
  • Lipid panel + fasting glucose / HbA1c — baseline and annually on ADT and AR-pathway inhibitors.63
  • Calcium and 25-OH vitamin D — baseline and periodically on ADT / bone-modifying agents. Replete; supplement Ca 1000–1200 mg + vit D 1000 IU daily.31
  • eGFR / creatinine — per agent (zoledronic acid, abiraterone, chemotherapy).3
  • Germline genetic testing — offer to all patients with advanced prostate cancer; add somatic tumor testing in metastatic disease (PARP inhibitor eligibility; cascade testing of relatives).3

Imaging

  • Multiparametric MRI prostate — every 1–3 years on active surveillance; confirmatory at 6–12 months. Stable PI-RADS; no new ≥PI-RADS 4 lesion.12
  • Confirmatory / surveillance prostate biopsy — MRI first if diagnostic biopsy was not MRI-guided; PI-RADS 4–5 → prompt targeted confirmatory biopsy; PI-RADS 1–3 → confirmatory biopsy within ~12 months. Then every 1–4 years (age, health, progression risk, preference) or as triggered. MRI does not replace periodic biopsy. No upgrading to Grade Group ≥2 (high-volume) or ≥3.21
  • DEXA / BMD on ADT — baseline DXA + FRAX before / at ADT start; re-assess FRAX + DXA 1 year after starting ADT / systemic therapy (greatest loss in year 1), then every 1–2 years per risk. FRAX 10-yr hip ≥3% or major osteoporotic ≥20% triggers bone-protective therapy (bisphosphonate or denosumab). Calcium, vit D, smoking cessation, weight-bearing exercise for all on ADT.31
  • Conventional imaging (CT abd/pelvis + Tc-99m bone scan) — at biochemical recurrence; at progression; every 6–12 months in mCRPC.31
  • nmCRPC imaging (rising PSA, castrate testosterone, no metastases) — conventional or PSMA PET imaging every 6–12 months; may extend to annually once on ARPI without other progression indicators. PSA every 3–6 months with PSADT calculation.3
  • PSMA PET/CT — initial staging of unfavorable intermediate / high-risk disease: PSMA PET OR bone scan + cross-sectional imaging (AUA/ASTRO 2026, strong); PSMA PET may be added if conventional imaging is negative; not routine in asymptomatic low / favorable-intermediate risk. PSA recurrence after exhaustion of local therapy: PSMA PET preferred (greater sensitivity) or when conventional imaging is negative (AUA/SUO 2026). Periodic restaging in advanced disease.23
  • Imaging in mCRPC without PSA progression — at least annually.3

Physical examination

  • DRE on active surveillance — annually (NCCN; AUA/ASTRO 2026 allows every 1–2 years — annual retained as more conservative). No new nodularity.12
  • DRE post-curative treatment — annually (may omit if PSA undetectable post-RP).16
  • Blood pressure / cardiovascular assessment — every visit on ADT or AR-pathway inhibitor.36

Functional / surveys

  • Urinary continence / LUTS assessment (EPIC, IPSS) — every follow-up visit, particularly first 2 years post-treatment.62
  • Erectile / sexual function (SHIM / IIEF) — at baseline and periodically. Offer PDE5i, penile rehabilitation, referral.62
  • Distress / depression / PSA-anxiety screen — initial visit and at appropriate intervals (PHQ-9 or NCCN Distress Thermometer).6
  • Hot flash / vasomotor symptom assessment — each visit on ADT. Options: venlafaxine, gabapentin, medroxyprogesterone.6
  • Bowel function — each visit post-RT. Refer for persistent rectal bleeding or urgency.6
  • Age-appropriate second-cancer screening (CRC, skin) — per USPSTF.67

Notes

  • PSA screening framework disagreement. USPSTF 2018 grade C (shared decision 55–69) and grade D (≥70) is more restrictive than AUA/SUO Early Detection (2023; amended 2026), which endorses baseline PSA at 45–50 and risk-stratified screening through age 70+ in healthy men. USPSTF update in progress (no draft posted as of Nov 2026).54
  • Active-surveillance biopsy trigger. NCCN relies on PSA kinetics and MRI changes; AUA emphasizes MRI plus periodic surveillance biopsy — MRI does not replace biopsy.12
  • PSMA PET utilization. AUA/ASTRO 2026 makes PSMA PET an equal alternative to conventional imaging for staging unfavorable intermediate / high-risk disease; AUA/SUO 2026 makes it the preferred modality at PSA recurrence after local therapy. CMS coverage is broad for FDA-approved tracers, but local contractor policies vary.23
  • Imaging in mCRPC without PSA progression. AUA requires at least annual imaging; NCCN allows symptom- and PSA-driven imaging, creating a real-world cadence gap.31
  • AS PSA interval and post-treatment PSA cadence. NCCN uses PSA every 3–6 months on active surveillance; AUA/ASTRO 2026 advises no more often than every 6 months — flagged, NCCN cadence retained. After curative therapy, AUA/ASTRO 2026 (q3–6 mo × 2 yr, q6 mo yr 2–5) is more frequent than NCCN / ACS (q6–12 mo × 5 yr) — AUA cadence adopted.126

Non-Alzheimer's Dementia

ICD-10: F01.x (vascular dementia), F02.x (dementia in other diseases — DLB, FTD), G31.x (other degenerative diseases — DLB, FTD, PSP) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 8.7% — rank 18 of 30 Category: Neurologic Primary associations consulted: AAN, AGS, APA, USPSTF, CMS, Alzheimer's Association

CCW separates Alzheimer's disease (rank 22) from non-Alzheimer's dementia. This monograph covers vascular dementia, dementia with Lewy bodies (DLB), frontotemporal dementia (FTD), and mixed dementias. Surveillance overlaps substantially with Alzheimer's care; subtype-specific cautions are flagged.

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — — Caregiver burden assessment (Zarit) every 6–12 mo TSH, vitamin B12 at diagnosis (and as clinically indicated); CMP / CBC for delirium workup — Repeat labs only with acute cognitive worsening (rule out delirium)
Imaging — — — — — Non-contrast MRI preferred (CT if MRI contraindicated) once at diagnosis; repeat only with atypical course or focal change
Physical exam Medication review with AGS Beers Criteria (anticholinergic burden, sedative-hypnotics, antipsychotics); antipsychotic appropriateness review if on agent — — Fall-risk assessment (Timed Up & Go, gait / balance); driving safety evaluation (CDR-based) — Antipsychotic taper attempt within 4 months of initiation (APA)
Functional / surveys Neuropsychiatric / behavioral symptom screen (NPI-Q, BEHAVE-AD, PHQ-2); vascular risk-factor control (BP, lipids, glycemia, smoking, AFib anticoagulation) — — Cognitive testing (MoCA, Mini-Cog, MMSE, or SLUMS); ADL / IADL (Katz / Lawton-Brody); dementia staging (FAST or CDR); depression screen (GDS-15 or PHQ-9); advance care planning / goals-of-care discussion; CMS Cognitive Assessment & Care Plan Service (CPT 99483, billable every 180 days) — Health-related social needs (HRSN) screening; GUIDE Model enrollment for community-dwelling patients

Summary recommendation

US guidelines converge on a structured longitudinal surveillance pattern for established non-Alzheimer's dementia anchored by the annual Medicare Annual Wellness Visit (mandated cognitive impairment detection) and, when indicated, the CMS Cognitive Assessment & Care Plan Service (CPT 99483, billable every 180 days), which packages the core surveillance bundle.8 The bundle includes cognitive testing (MoCA / Mini-Cog / MMSE / SLUMS), ADL / IADL functional review, FAST or CDR staging, neuropsychiatric symptom screen (NPI-Q, BEHAVE-AD, or PHQ-2), high-risk medication reconciliation under AGS Beers 2023 (anticholinergics, benzodiazepines, antipsychotics), home and driving safety evaluation, caregiver-needs assessment, advance care planning, and a written care plan.85 AAN practice parameters (dementia diagnosis 2001, reaffirmed 2024; MCI 2018; driving 2010) and the 2024 Alzheimer's Association DETeCD-ADRD guideline support cognitive testing at least annually, depression screening (GDS / PHQ-9), TSH / B12 at diagnosis, and one-time structural MRI / CT.16 The APA 2016 antipsychotic guideline frames behavioral-symptom management with non-pharmacologic first-line care, low-dose second-generation antipsychotics only for severe / dangerous symptoms, attempted taper within 4 months, and avoidance in DLB due to neuroleptic sensitivity.4 CMS's GUIDE Model (July 2024–2032) layers care navigation, 24/7 caregiver support, respite, and HRSN screening on top of the visit-based surveillance.9 Fall risk (TUG), driving risk (CDR-based), and vascular risk-factor control receive distinct emphasis in vascular and DLB phenotypes.

Laboratory

  • TSH, vitamin B12 — at diagnosis to identify reversible contributors; repeat as clinically indicated. Target B12 >300 pg/mL; TSH within reference range.16
  • CMP, CBC — at diagnosis; with acute cognitive change to rule out delirium.1

Imaging

  • Structural neuroimaging (non-contrast MRI preferred; CT if MRI contraindicated) — once at diagnosis. Identify vascular burden (white matter, infarcts) in vascular dementia, focal atrophy in FTD, relatively preserved hippocampi in DLB. Repeat only with atypical course or focal change.16

Physical examination

  • Medication review with AGS Beers Criteria — every visit (required element of CPT 99483). Avoid / deprescribe anticholinergics, benzodiazepines, Z-drugs, first-generation antihistamines; address cumulative anticholinergic burden. Use the AGS 2025 Beers Alternatives guidance for non-pharmacologic-first and safer pharmacologic substitutes; identify the underlying cause of the symptom and monitor for recurrence after withdrawal.5811
  • Antipsychotic appropriateness and deprescribing review — every visit if on agent. Taper attempt within 4 months of initiation. CAUTION: avoid antipsychotics in DLB (severe neuroleptic sensitivity).45
  • Fall-risk assessment (Timed Up & Go, gait / balance exam) — annually. TUG >12 seconds = increased risk. High priority in vascular (gait apraxia) and DLB (parkinsonism).26
  • Driving safety evaluation (CDR-based) — at diagnosis and annually; sooner if caregiver concern. CDR ≥1 = high risk, recommend cessation; CDR 0.5–1 = on-road evaluation. Particular relevance for DLB (fluctuating cognition) and FTD (impulsivity).3

Functional / surveys

  • Cognitive status testing (MoCA, MMSE, Mini-Cog, or SLUMS) — annually (every visit if change reported); CMS AWV requires annual detection. Decline >2–3 points/year suggests faster progression.2106
  • ADL (Katz) and IADL (Lawton-Brody) — annually and at any reported change; required at CPT 99483. IADL loss marks MCI-to-mild-dementia; ADL loss marks moderate-to-severe.81
  • Dementia staging (FAST or CDR) — annually; at each CPT 99483 encounter. CDR: 0.5 MCI; 1 mild; 2 moderate; 3 severe.83
  • Neuropsychiatric / behavioral symptom screen (NPI-Q, BEHAVE-AD, or PHQ-2) — every visit; required at CPT 99483. Especially relevant in DLB (visual hallucinations, REM sleep behavior disorder), FTD (disinhibition, apathy), vascular (apathy, depression).84
  • Depression screening (GDS-15 or PHQ-9) — annually and at change. PHQ-9 ≥10 warrants treatment. High comorbidity in vascular dementia.16
  • Caregiver burden assessment (Zarit Burden Interview) and caregiver support needs — at diagnosis, then every 6–12 months; ongoing under GUIDE. Zarit ≥21 = moderate burden; trigger respite, education, referral.96
  • Advance care planning / goals of care — at diagnosis, then annually and at stage transitions; required at CPT 99483.89
  • Health-related social needs (HRSN) and psychosocial screening — at enrollment and periodically (GUIDE-enrolled).9
  • Vascular risk-factor control (BP, lipids, glycemia, smoking, AFib anticoagulation) — every visit. BP <130/80 (per ACC/AHA); statin per ASCVD; A1c individualized. High priority in vascular dementia / secondary stroke prevention.26

Notes

  • Population screening. USPSTF 2020 issued an I-statement (insufficient evidence) on screening asymptomatic community-dwelling adults ≥65 (update in progress; research plan finalized 2025, I-statement remains operative)7; CMS nonetheless mandates AWV cognitive impairment detection; AAN and Alzheimer's Association endorse evaluation of symptomatic patients and brief tools when concerns arise. Ongoing tension between population screening and case-finding.710
  • Antipsychotic boundaries. FDA black-box warning (increased mortality in dementia) coexists with APA conditional recommendations for severe / dangerous symptoms; AGS Beers 2023 lists antipsychotics as "avoid" for behavioral problems unless non-pharmacologic options fail. APA permits short-term use with taper; Beers leans more restrictive.45
  • Cholinesterase inhibitors / memantine in non-AD dementias. AAN MCI 2018 recommends against pharmacologic cognitive enhancers in MCI; for DLB and Parkinson's dementia, rivastigmine has labeled evidence; for vascular and FTD, AAN does not endorse routine use.2
  • Routine repeat neuroimaging. AAN endorses one-time structural imaging at diagnosis; no guideline supports surveillance imaging absent clinical change. An AAN Etiologic Diagnosis of Dementia guideline is in draft (public comment); the 2001 parameter remains operative until it is finalized.1
