Lung Cancer

ICD-10: C34.x (malignant neoplasm of bronchus and lung) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 1.3% — rank 27 of 30 Category: Cancer Primary associations consulted: NCCN, USPSTF, CMS, ASCO, ATS, ACCP

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — irAE labs (TSH, LFTs, glucose, cortisol) prior to each immunotherapy infusion; response assessment imaging every 6–12 wk on active systemic therapy — Broad molecular profiling at diagnosis of advanced / metastatic nonsquamous NSCLC (EGFR, ALK, ROS1, BRAF, KRAS G12C, MET exon 14, RET, NTRK, ERBB2 / HER2 mutation, NRG1, PD-L1 IHC; HER2 and c-Met IHC overexpression); repeat at progression — Circulating biomarkers (ctDNA, CEA) NOT recommended for routine surveillance
Imaging — — Chest CT with contrast every 6 mo × 2–3 yr (stage I-II NSCLC); every 6 mo × 3 yr (stage III NSCLC); ASCO 2019 every 6 mo × 2 yr; chest CT every 2–6 mo × 1 yr then every 3–6 mo × 2–3 yr (SCLC); brain MRI every 3–4 mo × 1 yr then every 6 mo × 2 yr (limited-stage SCLC) Annual low-dose non-contrast chest CT after initial 2–3 yr surveillance window; annual LDCT lung-cancer screening for high-risk adults 50–80 yr with 20+ pack-year history (USPSTF Grade B; CMS NCD 210.14 ages 50–77, requires one-time shared decision-making visit / G0296) — Brain MRI NOT recommended routinely in stage I-III NSCLC (use only when symptomatic); PET / CT NOT recommended for routine surveillance
Physical exam Dyspnea and cough assessment — H&P + chest CT every 6 mo × 2–3 yr H&P annually after initial window — Dental / oral evaluation prior to thoracic RT; periodic on bone-modifying agents (zoledronic acid, denosumab for bone metastases)
Functional / surveys Smoking-cessation counseling + pharmacotherapy (combined behavioral + pharmacotherapy); distress screening (NCCN Distress Thermometer) — — Cancer-related fatigue screening; depression / anxiety (PHQ-9, GAD-7); chemotherapy-induced peripheral neuropathy (post-platinum / taxane); dietary counseling / weight management; age-appropriate other-cancer screening per USPSTF; vaccinations per ACIP — Pulmonary rehabilitation pre-op (prehab) and post-op (6–12 wk program) for lung resection; symptomatic survivors with dyspnea / deconditioning

Summary recommendation

Post-curative NSCLC surveillance per NCCN NSCLC v.1.2027 consists of H&P plus contrast-enhanced chest CT every 6 months for the first 2–3 years (longer interval for stage III), transitioning to annual low-dose non-contrast chest CT thereafter for life; ASCO 2019 endorses the same 6-mo × 2 yr then annual schedule.15 PET / CT and routine circulating biomarkers are explicitly NOT recommended for asymptomatic surveillance, and routine brain MRI is not recommended in stage I-III NSCLC (reserve for symptoms). Limited-stage SCLC requires intensified surveillance: chest CT every 2–6 months and brain MRI every 3–4 months in year 1, every 6 months in year 2 (NCCN SCLC v.1.2027).2 Metastatic NSCLC on active therapy requires response imaging every 6–12 weeks plus comprehensive molecular profiling at diagnosis (EGFR, ALK, ROS1, BRAF, KRAS G12C, MET ex14, RET, NTRK, HER2 mutation, NRG1, PD-L1; plus HER2 IHC 3+ and c-Met IHC overexpression)10 to guide targeted therapy and IO selection.1 Population screening with annual LDCT applies to adults 50–80 with ≥20 pack-years currently smoking or quit ≤15 years (USPSTF 2021 Grade B; CMS NCD 210.14, 2022, requires a one-time shared decision-making visit / G0296).34 Survivorship care addresses smoking cessation at every visit (combined behavioral + pharmacotherapy), pulmonary rehabilitation pre / post-resection, surveillance for platinum-induced peripheral neuropathy, screening for depression, fatigue, distress, dyspnea, dietary counseling, and age-appropriate other-cancer screening.6

Laboratory

  • Broad molecular profiling — EGFR (incl. exon 19 del / L858R, exon 20 ins, uncommon S768I / L861Q / G719X), ALK, ROS1, BRAF V600E, KRAS (incl G12C), MET exon 14 skipping, RET, NTRK1/2/3, ERBB2 (HER2) mutation, NRG1 fusion; PD-L1 IHC — at diagnosis of advanced / metastatic nonsquamous NSCLC, before starting systemic therapy. Protein-overexpression IHC with therapeutic implications: HER2 IHC 3+ (fam-trastuzumab deruxtecan, subsequent line) and c-Met ≥50% IHC 3+ in EGFR wild-type (telisotuzumab vedotin). Repeat tissue / plasma ctDNA testing at progression (resistance mechanisms). Select squamous (never / light smokers, small biopsy).10
  • PD-L1 IHC (tumor proportion score) — at diagnosis; repeat at progression. Inform pembrolizumab / IO selection.1
  • Immune-related adverse event labs (TSH, LFTs, glucose, cortisol) — prior to each immunotherapy infusion.8
  • Circulating biomarkers (ctDNA, CEA) — NOT recommended for routine surveillance.5

Imaging

  • Chest CT with contrast (NSCLC post-curative) — every 6 mo for 2–3 yr; longer interval (every 6 mo × 3 yr) for stage III; transition to annual low-dose non-contrast CT for life.15
  • Chest CT (SCLC post-curative) — every 2–3 mo × yr 1, every 3–6 mo × yr 2–3, every 6 mo × yr 4, then annually.2
  • Brain MRI (preferred) or brain CT with contrast (SCLC) — every 3–4 mo × yr 1, every 6 mo × yr 2 (regardless of PCI status).2
  • Brain MRI in NSCLC — NOT recommended routinely in stage I-III; use only when symptomatic.5
  • PET / CT — NOT recommended for routine surveillance.51
  • Low-dose CT (LDCT) lung-cancer screening — annual for high-risk adults 50–80 yr, 20+ pack-year history, currently smoking or quit ≤15 yr (USPSTF Grade B). CMS NCD 210.14 covers ages 50–77 with one-time shared decision-making visit (G0296).34
  • Response assessment imaging (chest / abdomen CT ± brain MRI) — every 6–12 wk during active systemic therapy (drug- and protocol-specific) for metastatic NSCLC and extensive-stage SCLC. RECIST response.12

Physical examination

  • Dyspnea and cough assessment — at every clinical encounter for all NSCLC / SCLC survivors and patients on active therapy.16
  • H&P + chest CT — per surveillance interval above.1
  • Dental / oral evaluation — baseline prior to thoracic radiation; periodically thereafter on bone-modifying agents (zoledronic acid, denosumab) for bone metastases.6

Functional / surveys

  • Smoking-cessation counseling + pharmacotherapy (combined behavioral + pharmacotherapy) — at every visit; every patient who smokes or has quit <12 mo. All lung cancer patients (any stage, any time point) and all screening candidates. NCCN Smoking Cessation v.2.2025 Category 1.74
  • Pulmonary rehabilitation — pre-operative (prehab) and post-operative; typical 6–12 wk program. Patients undergoing lung resection; symptomatic NSCLC survivors with dyspnea / deconditioning.9
  • Distress screening (NCCN Distress Thermometer) — at every visit; minimum at key transitions.6
  • Cancer-related fatigue screening — at every visit.6
  • Chemotherapy-induced peripheral neuropathy assessment — every visit during / after platinum or taxane therapy.6
  • Depression and anxiety screening (PHQ-9, GAD-7) — annually minimum; more often during active treatment.6
  • Dietary counseling / weight management — at each follow-up. Maintain healthy BMI; address sarcopenia.6
  • Age-appropriate other-cancer screening (CRC, breast, cervical, prostate per USPSTF) — per organ-specific guideline.6
  • Immunizations (influenza annual, pneumococcal, COVID-19, RSV per age, Tdap) — per ACIP schedule, particularly post-resection.6
  • Counseling and shared decision-making visit (G0296) — once, prior to first LDCT (Medicare-eligible high-risk population).4

Notes

  • CT surveillance interval, stage I NSCLC. NCCN allows every 6 mo for 2–3 yr (some institutions extending to 6–12 mo); ASCO 2019 specifies every 6 mo × 2 yr then annual. Observational data show no OS benefit comparing 3-, 6-, and 12-month intervals after stage I-III resection.15
  • LDCT vs. contrast CT for post-curative surveillance. NCCN transitions to annual LDCT non-contrast after the initial high-frequency window; some institutions continue contrast-enhanced CT lifelong, especially for stage III.1
  • PET / CT. ASCO and NCCN agree PET / CT should not be used for routine asymptomatic surveillance, but practice patterns frequently include PET — a known guideline-practice gap.51
  • Brain MRI for stage III NSCLC. ASCO 2019 does not recommend routine surveillance brain MRI in stage I-III NSCLC; some thoracic oncology programs perform baseline and surveillance brain MRI in stage III given higher CNS relapse risk, particularly after chemoradiation and durvalumab consolidation.5
  • Upper age limit and eligibility for screening. USPSTF (Grade B, 2021) and CMS NCD 210.14 cap at 80 and 77 respectively; both require quit ≤15 yr. FLAG: ACS (2023) screens current or former smokers 50–80 with ≥20 pack-years regardless of years since quitting; NCCN Lung Cancer Screening (v.1.2027) is broader still. Per synthesis rule, USPSTF / CMS criteria govern coverage and default eligibility.341112
  • ctDNA / MRD testing. Not endorsed by ASCO 2019 or NCCN as routine surveillance, but increasing institutional and trial use for molecular residual disease detection.51

Hip / Pelvic Fracture

ICD-10: S72.x (hip fracture), S32.x (pelvic fracture), M84.x (pathologic fracture), Z87.81 (personal history of pathologic fracture) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 1.2% — rank 28 of 30 Category: Musculoskeletal Primary associations consulted: AAOS, ASBMR, BHOF, AGS, ACS GSV, CDC STEADI, CMS

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — — — 25-OH vitamin D (post-repletion); calcium intake assessment — Baseline secondary-causes labs (Ca, 25-OH vit D, PTH, alk phos, Cr / eGFR, Mg, TSH, CBC, 24-hr urine Ca) before anti-resorptive initiation; serum Ca + eGFR prior to IV zoledronate / denosumab dose; denosumab with advanced CKD / dialysis: serum Ca closely weeks 2–10 post-injection (FDA boxed warning); CTX / P1NP for adherence + response 3–6 mo after therapy start
Imaging — — — DEXA every 1–2 yr on therapy (AACE) or 1–3 yr (Endo Society) until stable — Baseline DEXA at index hospitalization or 1st outpatient visit if not previously osteoporotic; targeted radiographs only when clinically indicated (pain, function decline, suspected nonunion / hardware failure); vertebral fracture assessment (VFA) per BHOF triggers
Physical exam Pain assessment; mobility / PT / OT progress Multidisciplinary rehabilitation (PT, OT, SLP as indicated) — daily IRF (≥3 hr/day); SNF therapy ≥5 d/wk; outpatient PT ongoing — Standing height measurement; Timed Up and Go (TUG) screening; fall-risk reassessment via CDC STEADI Screen-Assess-Intervene — Comprehensive geriatric assessment (cognition, function, nutrition, goals of care, polypharmacy); CAM delirium screen daily / per shift through post-op hospital stay (age ≥75); surgical-site / hardware imaging only when clinically indicated
Functional / surveys Pain control; medication review for falls / orthostasis / sedation (AGS Beers Criteria) — Denosumab continuity check (no gap >7 mo) FRAX 10-yr fracture-risk recalculation; depression screen (PHQ-2 / PHQ-9) at 1, 3, 6, 12 mo PCP visits; secondary fracture risk score — FLS (Fracture Liaison Service) / Own the Bone enrollment during index hospitalization; anti-resorptive initiation (oral bisphosphonate first-line; IV zoledronic acid annual; denosumab q6 mo) within months of fracture; VTE prophylaxis (LMWH / DOAC / aspirin) minimum 10–14 days, extendable 28–35 days post-op

Summary recommendation

Post-hip / pelvic fracture surveillance in adults ≥65 follows a phased trajectory anchored by interdisciplinary co-management.12 Acute inpatient and IRF care (Medicare Part A, no 3-day rule for IRF) includes ACS-GSV-aligned daily delirium screening, multimodal pain control, 10–35-day VTE prophylaxis, and ≥3 hr/day therapy.1012 Post-discharge SNF coverage (up to 100 days / benefit period; cost share after day 20 in 2026) or home-health continues PT / OT, with primary-care visits typically at 1, 3, 6, and 12 months.12 At those visits, clinicians repeat the CDC STEADI fall-risk screen (TUG <12 s target), apply the 2023 AGS Beers Criteria to deprescribe sedatives, anticholinergics, orthostasis-inducing agents, and other fall-risk drugs, and assess vitamin D and calcium adequacy (1,000–1,200 mg Ca / day; vitamin D supplementation in adults ≥75).711 If not previously diagnosed, DEXA is obtained (USPSTF 2025 grade B in women ≥65 and high-risk postmenopausal women).4 The ASBMR Secondary Fracture Prevention coalition, BHOF, and AOA's Own the Bone program direct prompt anti-resorptive initiation (IV zoledronic acid annually — strongest mortality / fracture-reduction evidence from HORIZON-RFT; oral alendronate or risedronate first-line; denosumab when bisphosphonates contraindicated), ideally coordinated through a Fracture Liaison Service.28 Ongoing surveillance includes depression screening, cognition monitoring, FRAX recalculation, and serial BMD on therapy.

Laboratory

  • Secondary-causes workup at index hospitalization — Ca, 25-OH vit D, PTH, alk phos, Cr / eGFR, Mg, TSH, CBC, 24-hr urine Ca; consider SPEP, celiac serology, testosterone (men).23
  • 25-OH vitamin D — baseline; repeat after repletion; vitamin D supplementation in adults ≥75 (Endocrine Society 2024 no longer endorses a universal 30 ng/mL threshold for healthy adults <75; ASBMR / BHOF still advise sufficiency).92
  • Serum calcium (corrected) + Cr / eGFR — before each IV zoledronate or denosumab dose. Denosumab in advanced CKD / dialysis (FDA boxed warning): assess for CKD-MBD before starting / continuing (nephrology input), correct hypocalcemia, supplement calcium + active vitamin D, and monitor serum Ca closely after each injection — highest risk weeks 2–10 (peak weeks 2–5).1326
  • Bone-turnover markers (CTX or P1NP) — baseline; 3–6 months after starting / changing therapy; during drug holiday.63

Imaging

  • Central DEXA / BMD — baseline post-fracture if not previously diagnosed (USPSTF 2025 B in women ≥65). Repeat every 1–2 years on therapy (AACE) or 1–3 years (Endocrine Society) until stable.465
  • Targeted radiographs — only when clinically indicated (pain, function decline, suspected nonunion / hardware failure). Not routine.1
  • Vertebral fracture assessment (VFA) via DXA or lateral spine radiograph — at baseline if height loss ≥1.5 in historical or ≥0.8 in prospective, kyphosis, chronic glucocorticoids, or T-score ≤ −2.5.36

Physical examination

  • Standing height measurement (stadiometer) — annually. Trigger VFA if loss ≥2 cm prospective or ≥4 cm historical.3
  • Timed Up and Go (TUG) — at rehab discharge, then annually or with status change. TUG <12 sec (STEADI threshold); ≥12 sec increased fall risk.7
  • CDC STEADI Screen-Assess-Intervene (fall history, gait / balance, postural BP) — at every primary-care visit after fracture; minimum annually.72
  • Interdisciplinary functional assessment with PT / OT — daily during inpatient stay / IRF; multiple sessions/week in SNF; periodic outpatient follow-up.110
  • CAM (or equivalent) delirium screen — minimum daily / per shift in adults ≥75 through post-op hospital stay; reassess at discharge.10
  • Multimodal pain assessment — each visit during rehab and primary-care follow-up. Minimize opioids / Beers-listed agents.111

Functional / surveys

  • AGS Beers Criteria deprescribing review (fall-risk meds, orthostasis, sedation) — at each transition of care and at least annually. Discontinue / reduce benzodiazepines, anticholinergics, sedating antidepressants, alpha-blockers.11
  • Calcium intake — ongoing. Target 1,000–1,200 mg / day.23
  • Anti-resorptive initiation — promptly post-fracture (within months). Zoledronic acid annual; denosumab q6 mo; oral bisphosphonate weekly / monthly. HORIZON: ≥2 weeks post-op (median ~6 wk); 35% fracture risk reduction, 28% mortality reduction with zoledronic acid.28
  • Fracture Liaison Service (FLS) / Own the Bone enrollment — during index hospitalization; coordinator follow-up over 1 yr. Target ≥80% of fracture patients evaluated, treated, and adherent at 1 yr.23
  • VTE prophylaxis (LMWH, fondaparinux, DOAC, warfarin, or aspirin) — minimum 10–14 days; extend up to 28–35 days post-op.1
  • Depression screening (PHQ-2 / PHQ-9) — at rehab transition and at 1, 3, 6, 12 mo primary-care visits.2
  • FRAX 10-year fracture-risk recalculation — post-fracture and on follow-up; with each BMD.32
  • Comprehensive geriatric / orthogeriatric assessment — pre-op (when feasible), in-hospital, and post-discharge.101

Notes

  • Anti-resorptive timing. HORIZON-RFT supports zoledronic acid at ≥2 weeks post-op (median ~6 wk); some orthopaedic groups historically delayed bisphosphonates 3 months over fracture-healing concerns — now largely refuted. AAOS does not specify exact start; ASBMR / BHOF recommend "prompt" initiation within months.82
  • Vitamin D target. 2024 Endocrine Society guideline no longer endorses 25-OH-D ≥30 ng/mL and discourages empiric testing in healthy adults <75; ASBMR 2019 and BHOF still advise sufficiency (≥30 ng/mL, with ≥20 ng/mL as a more conservative threshold per IOM).92
  • FLS coverage. No dedicated CMS bundled benefit; reimbursement is through component E/M, care-management codes, and DEXA — a recognized gap in BHOF policy commentary.3
  • Drug-of-choice. ASBMR lists alendronate / risedronate as first-line on cost / tolerability; HORIZON-RFT gives zoledronic acid a unique mortality-reduction advantage.28
  • TUG cutoff. STEADI / general fall screening uses ≥12 sec; post-hip-fracture-specific literature has used 24 sec to predict 6-month falls.7

Endometrial Cancer

ICD-10: C54.x (malignant neoplasm of corpus uteri) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 1.1% (females only) — rank 29 of 30 Category: Cancer (females only) Primary associations consulted: NCCN, SGO, ACOG, USPSTF, ACS

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — Serum CA-125 only if elevated at baseline / diagnosis (otherwise NOT routine) — — — Universal MMR IHC (± MLH1 methylation reflex) ± MSI on tumor at diagnosis for Lynch syndrome screening; germline genetic counseling / testing if MMR-deficient (not MLH1 methylation) or strong family history; irAE labs (TSH, LFTs, glucose, cortisol) baseline + prior to each cycle of checkpoint inhibitor (pembrolizumab / dostarlimab)
Imaging — H&P + pelvic exam every 3–6 mo × 2–3 yr (stage III/IV or serous / clear cell every 3 mo) H&P + pelvic exam every 6 mo through yr 5 (after initial high-frequency window); CT chest / abdomen / pelvis every 6 mo × 3 yr (stage III-IV, serous / clear cell, carcinosarcoma) — — Vaginal / pelvic MRI or CT as clinically indicated for symptoms or abnormal exam; response imaging CT chest / abd / pelvis every 2–3 cycles (≈ every 9–12 wk) during active systemic therapy for metastatic / recurrent disease
Physical exam — H&P with speculum + bimanual pelvic exam every 3–6 mo first 2–3 yr H&P every 6 mo through yr 5 H&P annually after yr 5; lymphedema surveillance + education (post-pelvic lymphadenectomy / pelvic RT) — —
Functional / surveys Patient education on recurrence symptoms (vaginal bleeding, pelvic / abdominal pain, cough, weight loss, leg swelling); distress / depression / anxiety screening (NCCN Distress Thermometer) — — Lifestyle counseling (weight management, exercise, healthy diet, smoking cessation — endometrial cancer is metabolically driven); cardiovascular risk management (BP, lipids, diabetes screening); vasomotor symptom management (non-hormonal preferred; HT case-by-case); sexual health / vaginal health (moisturizers, dilators after vaginal brachytherapy); bone health (DXA per osteoporosis guidelines, especially if premenopausal at oophorectomy); age-appropriate other-cancer screening (breast, cervical per ACS / USPSTF, colorectal — accelerated to age 20–25 q1–2 yr if Lynch+) — Vaginal cuff cytology / Pap NOT recommended routinely (discontinued in current NCCN — symptom-driven evaluation only)

Summary recommendation

US post-treatment surveillance for endometrial cancer is driven primarily by NCCN Uterine Neoplasms v3.2026, the SGO post-treatment surveillance clinical practice statement (Gynecol Oncol 2026), and the 2026 SGO endometrial cancer evidence-based review (Parts I–II), which are aligned.12512 H&P with speculum and bimanual pelvic exam is performed every 3–6 months for the first 2–3 years and then every 6–12 months through year 5 (annually thereafter), with frequency stratified by risk: stage IA grade 1–2 disease at the lower end (every 6 months) and stage III-IV or serous / clear cell histology every 3 months for 2–3 years. Routine vaginal cuff cytology has been discontinued — it detects <1% of recurrences, is cost-ineffective, and both NCCN and SGO (Choosing Wisely) explicitly recommend against it; evaluation is symptom- and exam-driven. Routine surveillance CT is NOT recommended for low-risk stage I disease but IS recommended every 6 months for years 1–3 in stage III-IV, serous / clear cell, or carcinosarcoma. Serum CA-125 is reserved for patients with elevated baseline levels (TOTEM trial showed no benefit in routine use).1 Universal Lynch syndrome tumor screening with MMR IHC ± MSI is recommended at diagnosis for all newly diagnosed endometrial cancers regardless of age (NCCN, SGO, ACOG).134 Survivorship care emphasizes lifestyle counseling (weight, exercise, diabetes, lipids), vasomotor and sexual health management, lymphedema surveillance, bone health after surgical menopause, distress screening, and age-appropriate other-cancer screening. USPSTF has issued no recommendation for endometrial cancer screening in asymptomatic average-risk women. Patients on active systemic therapy for metastatic / recurrent disease receive response imaging every 9–12 weeks and irAE labs with checkpoint inhibitor regimens.

Laboratory

  • Universal MMR IHC (± MLH1 methylation reflex) ± MSI — at diagnosis on all newly diagnosed endometrial cancers regardless of age or family history. Identify germline mutation carriers.134
  • Germline genetic counseling / testing — if MMR-deficient on IHC (not due to MLH1 methylation) or strong family history. Cascade testing for Lynch carriers.1
  • Serum CA-125 — only if elevated at baseline / diagnosis; otherwise NOT routine. Reserved for serous, clear cell, or any histology with elevated preop CA-125. TOTEM trial showed no benefit in routine use.12
  • ctDNA / MRD assays — NOT recommended for routine surveillance outside trials (SGO 2026: requires additional study).2
  • Immune-related adverse event labs (TSH, LFTs, glucose, cortisol) — baseline and prior to each cycle on checkpoint inhibitor (pembrolizumab / dostarlimab).1

Imaging

  • Surveillance CT chest / abdomen / pelvis — every 6 months for first 3 years, then every 6–12 months for 2 additional years (consider) for stage III-IV, serous / clear cell, carcinosarcoma, or metastatic on systemic therapy. NOT routine for stage I, grade 1–2 disease.1
  • Vaginal / pelvic MRI or CT — as clinically indicated for symptoms or abnormal exam.1
  • Response imaging CT chest / abd / pelvis — every 2–3 cycles (≈ every 9–12 wk) during active systemic therapy for metastatic / recurrent disease.1

Physical examination

  • H&P with pelvic exam (speculum + bimanual) — every 3–6 mo for first 2–3 yr (stage III/IV or serous / clear cell every 3 mo); every 6 mo through yr 5; then annually. Risk-stratified to lower frequency for stage IA grade 1–2.12
  • Lymphedema surveillance + education — assess at each visit; refer to lymphedema therapy as needed (post pelvic ± para-aortic lymphadenectomy or pelvic RT).12
  • Vaginal cuff cytology / Pap — NOT recommended routinely (discontinued); symptom-driven evaluation only.12

Functional / surveys

  • Patient education on recurrence symptoms — at every visit. Vaginal bleeding, pelvic / abdominal pain, cough, weight loss, leg swelling.12
  • Lifestyle counseling — weight management, exercise, healthy diet — at each follow-up. Especially BMI ≥30, type 2 DM, metabolic syndrome (endometrial cancer is metabolically driven).1
  • Smoking cessation counseling — every visit.1
  • Cardiovascular risk management (BP, lipids, diabetes screening) — per primary care, at least annually.1
  • Vasomotor symptom management — as needed; non-hormonal preferred; hormone therapy case-by-case.1
  • Sexual health / vaginal health — assessed at each visit; moisturizers, dilators after vaginal brachytherapy / radiation; low-dose vaginal estrogen case-by-case.12
  • Bone health — DXA scan — baseline and per osteoporosis guidelines; consider in premenopausal women rendered menopausal by surgery / RT.1
  • Distress / depression / anxiety screening (NCCN Distress Thermometer) — at each visit.1
  • Age-appropriate cancer screening — breast, cervical per ACS / USPSTF, colorectal (accelerated to age 20–25 q1–2 yr if Lynch+).1

Notes

  • Vaginal cuff cytology. Historical practice (pre-2011) routinely included annual Pap of the vaginal cuff; current NCCN, SGO, ESGO, and ACOG all recommend against it — but it remains in some community settings.12
  • Surveillance imaging in high-risk disease. NCCN endorses routine CT every 6 months for 3 years in stage III-IV / serous / clear cell; ESMO and some SGO experts favor a more individualized, symptom-driven approach citing limited survival data. SGO's surveillance statement (Gynecol Oncol 2026) finds symptom review and physical exam the most effective recurrence-detection tools, vaginal cytology of negligible benefit, routine imaging without proven benefit outside specific settings, and ctDNA / novel serum biomarkers not yet ready for routine use.12
  • Lynch syndrome screening. NCCN and SGO now recommend universal MMR tumor testing at diagnosis regardless of age; older ACOG / SGO algorithms had offered alternative age-based or family-history-based pathways. Universal testing is now the dominant US standard.13
  • Post-treatment hormone therapy. ACOG / NAMS permit individualized systemic HT in early-stage low-grade survivors with severe vasomotor symptoms; NCCN takes a more cautious position favoring non-hormonal therapy first.1
  • ASCO endometrial-specific survivorship guideline. No ASCO endometrial / gynecologic-cancer survivorship guideline had been published as of Nov 2026 (ASCO's 2026 living guideline on recurrent ovarian cancer does not address endometrial cancer); ASCO guidance continues to defer to NCCN / SGO.1

Urologic Cancer

ICD-10: C67.x (bladder), C64-C66 (kidney / renal pelvis / ureter) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 0.9% — rank 30 of 30 Category: Cancer Primary associations consulted: AUA / SUO, AUA / ASCO / ASTRO / SUO, NCCN, ASCO, USPSTF

CCW classifies bladder cancer and renal cell carcinoma together as "urologic cancer" (excluding prostate, which is condition #17). Both have distinct surveillance schedules and are addressed separately.

Bladder Cancer (NMIBC + MIBC)

At-a-glance schedule (Bladder Cancer)

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — Urine cytology every 3–6 mo × 2 yr (intermediate / high-risk NMIBC); BCG response assessment cytology at 3 + 6 mo post-induction (high-risk on BCG); annual B12 starting 2–3 yr post-diversion (ileal conduit / neobladder); BMP / acid-base every 3–6 mo early postop, then annually (post-cystectomy diversions) Renal function (Cr / eGFR) + upper-tract imaging every 6 mo through yr 3 (post-cystectomy) Renal function + upper-tract imaging annually after yr 3 (post-cystectomy); B12 annually — Urinary biomarkers — may be used to adjudicate equivocal cytology and to assess / predict response to intravesical therapy; do NOT replace cystoscopy; not routine in low-risk patients with normal cystoscopy
Imaging — Cystoscopy every 3–4 mo × 2 yr (high-risk NMIBC); every 3–6 mo × 2 yr (intermediate-risk); CT chest + CT / MRI abd / pelvis every 6–12 mo × 2–3 yr (post-cystectomy for MIBC) Cystoscopy every 6 mo yrs 3–4 (high / intermediate-risk NMIBC); CT chest + abd / pelvis annually after yr 3 (post-cystectomy) Cystoscopy annually after yr 4–5 (NMIBC); upper-tract imaging (CT / MR urography) every 1–2 yr (intermediate / high-risk NMIBC); cystoscopy first at 3–4 mo, then 6–9 mo, then annually (low-risk NMIBC) Shared decision-making to discontinue cystoscopy after 5 yr recurrence-free (low-risk NMIBC) Urethral wash cytology periodically if urethra retained (especially with prostatic urethral involvement); normal cystoscopy + positive high-grade cytology → upper-tract evaluation, random bladder biopsies, prostatic urethral biopsies (blue-light cystoscopy when available); routine bladder cancer screening NOT recommended in asymptomatic adults (USPSTF I-statement)
Physical exam — — — — — Sexual / urinary function and ostomy / neobladder care
Functional / surveys — — — — — —

Summary (Bladder Cancer)

For NMIBC, the AUA / SUO guideline (2016; amended 2020, 2024, 2026) recommends cystoscopy + cytology every 3–4 months × 2 years, every 6 months for years 3–4, then annually for high-risk disease, with upper-tract imaging (CT / MR urography) every 1–2 years; intermediate-risk uses every 3–6 month cystoscopy tapering to annual; low-risk requires cystoscopy at 3–4 months, 6–9 months, then annually, with no routine cytology or upper-tract imaging. The 2026 amendment revised intermediate- and high-risk categories: all high-grade Ta tumors are now high risk (cystoscopy + cytology q3–4 mo × 2 yr, q6 mo yrs 3–4, then annually, with upper-tract imaging q1–2 yr); intermediate risk is now chiefly low-grade disease that is large, multifocal, or recurrent.171 Post-cystectomy MIBC patients (AUA / ASCO / SUO 2024) require chest + abd / pelvis CT or MRI every 6–12 months × 2–3 years then annually, plus lifelong metabolic monitoring (annual B12 for ileal-segment diversions, BMP for acid-base) and renal function / upper-tract surveillance.2

Renal Cell Carcinoma

At-a-glance schedule (Renal Cell Carcinoma)

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — BMP / eGFR / CBC / LDH every 6 mo × 3 yr (stage II-III high-risk); response imaging on systemic therapy every 6–16 wk; irAE labs on ICI / TKI per regimen — BMP / Cr / eGFR / CBC / LDH annually (stage I); renal function annually (small renal mass on AS) — TSH / LFTs / BMP / CBC per ICI / TKI toxicity schedule; bone scan / brain MRI only if symptomatic (bone pain, ↑ alk phos, neurologic symptoms)
Imaging — Abdominal CT or MRI + chest CT every 3–6 mo × 3 yr (high-risk post-resection stage II-III); response imaging every 6–16 wk (stage IV on systemic therapy); abdominal imaging every 3–6 mo yr 1 (small renal mass on AS) — Abdominal imaging (CT / MRI; ultrasound acceptable after baseline) annually × 3 yr or longer (stage I post-resection); chest imaging (CXR or chest CT) annually × at least 5 yr; chest CT preferred annually × 5 yr (stage II-III high-risk after initial intensive window); abdominal imaging annually after yr 1 (small renal mass on AS) — —
Physical exam — H&P every 3–6 mo × 3 yr (stage II-III post-resection) — H&P annually (stage I); H&P annually to yr 5 (stage II-III); H&P + labs annually (stage I post-resection) — —
Functional / surveys — — — — — Renal-function preservation counseling (NSAID avoidance, contrast safety); active surveillance candidacy reassessment for small renal mass (growth <5 mm/yr; size <3–4 cm)

Summary (Renal Cell Carcinoma)

For RCC (AUA Renal Mass 2021; NCCN Kidney Cancer v.2.2027), stage I post-resection requires baseline imaging at 3–12 months then annually × 3+ years with annual chest imaging × 5 years; stage II-III requires every 3–6 month chest + abdominal CT × 3 years tapering to annual through year 5; small renal masses on active surveillance need imaging every 3–6 months in year 1 then every 6–12 months.34 Stage IV on systemic therapy requires response imaging every 6–16 weeks per regimen plus immunotherapy / TKI toxicity surveillance (TSH, LFTs, BMP, CBC).5

Combined Summary

US guidelines support intensive, risk-stratified surveillance for both major urologic cancers. NMIBC surveillance is anchored in cystoscopy + cytology every 3–4 months for years 1–2 in high-risk disease, tapering to annual after year 5; low-risk NMIBC is more permissive, with cytology and upper-tract imaging not routinely required. Post-cystectomy MIBC patients require chest + abd / pelvis CT or MRI every 6–12 months × 2–3 years then annually, plus annual B12 for ileal-segment diversions and renal-function / upper-tract surveillance. RCC post-resection cadence varies by stage (annual stage I; every 3–6 months × 3 years for stage II-III high-risk). Active surveillance for small renal mass requires baseline-to-frequent imaging early then annually. USPSTF gives bladder cancer screening in asymptomatic adults an "I" (insufficient evidence) statement.6

Notes

  • NMIBC low-risk intensity. AUA / SUO has progressively de-escalated low-risk surveillance (now allowing shared decision-making after 5 years recurrence-free). The 2024 AUA Amendment recommends against routine biomarker / cytology in low-risk patients with normal cystoscopy.1
  • Low-grade recurrence. The 2026 AUA / SUO amendment permits active surveillance, in-office fulguration, or intravesical chemoablation for recurrent low-grade NMIBC (Conditional / Grade C) and for small papillary recurrence after low-grade Ta (Expert Opinion), in place of TUR under anesthesia.1
  • Upper-tract imaging frequency. AUA recommends every 1–2 years for intermediate / high-risk NMIBC; NCCN / European guidance varies between 1-year and longer intervals based on prior upper-tract pathology.1
  • RCC active surveillance vs. intervention thresholds. AUA triggers intervention at tumor size >3–4 cm, growth >5 mm/year, or stage progression; NCCN is less prescriptive on absolute size cutoffs.34
  • Post-cystectomy B12 monitoring. Strongly recommended in clinical practice and AUA narrative guidance, but exact start time (immediate vs. 2–3 years postop) is not uniformly codified.2
  • ASCO RCC survivorship. No standalone ASCO RCC survivorship guideline exists; ASCO 2022 (with 2023 rapid recommendation update) covers metastatic clear-cell RCC management.58
